.

5
Guarded Pomsets

We now identify a fragment of the semantics that we use in the analysis of the litmus test from (
†
‣
1
), namely the
guarded
pomsets. This term comes from Jipsen and Moshier
[
10
]
, and was meant to define guarded pomsets in analogy to guarded strings in KAT.

We need two pieces of notation. First, we define the result of a state
after updating it for one value. Let
\mathtt{a}\in\mathsf{Act}
and
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
.
We say
that
\alpha[\mathtt{a}]

exists
if
\mathtt{a}=v\leftarrow n

for some
n\in\textsc{Val}
or
\mathtt{a}=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
.
If
\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
exists, we define it for all
w\in\textsc{Var}
as follows:

\alpha[v\leftarrow n](w)=\begin{cases}n&\text{if }w=v\\
\alpha(w)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\alpha[v\leftarrow v^{\prime}](w)=\begin{cases}\alpha(v^{\prime})&\text{if }w=v\\
\alpha(w)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Second, we define a binary operator
\oplus
on
\mathsf{State}
to combine states. For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{State}
:

\alpha\oplus\beta=\begin{cases}\alpha\cup\beta&\text{ if $\alpha(v)=\beta(v)$ for all }v\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)\cap\mathsf{dom}(\beta)\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Definition 5.27
.

The set of
guarded pomsets
, denoted
\mathcal{G}
, is the smallest set satisfying:

\frac{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}}{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\quad\mathtt{a}\in\mathsf{Act}\quad\alpha[\mathtt{a}]\text{ exists}}{\alpha\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot\alpha[\mathtt{a}]\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{U\cdot\alpha,\alpha\cdot V\in\mathcal{G}\quad\alpha\in\mathsf{State}}{U\cdot\alpha\cdot V\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\alpha\cdot U\cdot\beta\quad\gamma\cdot V\cdot\delta\in\mathcal{G}\quad\alpha\oplus\gamma\text{ defined }\quad\beta\oplus\delta\text{ defined }\quad\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathsf{State}}{\alpha\oplus\gamma\cdot(U\parallel V)\cdot\beta\oplus\delta\in\mathcal{G}}

This definition is close to
[
10
]
.
The definition in op. cit. is not catered to a specific alphabet, and the operator
\oplus
to combine states does not allow for
the two states to have any shared variable in their domains.
We deliberately deviate from this, allowing threads to share variables as long as they do so in a consistent manner.

Example 5.28
.

Consider the guarded pomsets
(x=1)\cdot(x\leftarrow 2)\cdot(x=2)
and
(x=1\wedge y=3)\cdot(x\leftarrow 2)\cdot(x=2\wedge y=3)\cdot(y\leftarrow x)\cdot(x=2\wedge y=2)
.
The final rule for the construction of
\mathcal{G}
guarantees that the following pomset is again guarded:

(x=1\wedge y=3)
(x\leftarrow 2)
(x=2\wedge y=3)
(y\leftarrow x)
(x=2\wedge y=2)
(x\leftarrow 2)

Note how
(x=2)\oplus(x=2\wedge y=3)
is defined, because both states agree on the value of
x
.

In the execution of parallel threads in pomsets, no interaction between the threads takes place: the threads execute “truly” concurrently.
To account for interactions, we consider the interleavings that result from closure w.r.t. the exchange law (c.f.
lemma

2.10
).

Example 5.29
.

Consider the a slightly adjusted version of the litmus test
t
discussed earlier:

t^{\prime}=(r_{0}=0\wedge r_{1}=0);(\textsf{T0}\parallel\textsf{T1});(r_{0}=1\vee r_{1}=1)

The
unclosed
semantics of
t^{\prime}
includes (but is not limited to) the pomset below on the left, for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathsf{State}
, where
\alpha(r_{0})=\alpha(r_{1})=0
, and
\gamma(r_{0})=1
or
\gamma(r_{1})=1
.
As a result of the exchange law, the
closed
semantics includes the pomset below on the right.

\alpha
(x\leftarrow 1)
(y\leftarrow 1)
\beta
(r_{0}\leftarrow y)
(r_{1}\leftarrow x)
\gamma
\alpha
(x\leftarrow 1)
(y\leftarrow 1)
\beta
(r_{0}\leftarrow y)
(r_{1}\leftarrow x)
\gamma

In the special case where
\alpha=\{r_{0}\mapsto 0,r_{1}\mapsto 0\}
,
\beta=\{r_{0}\mapsto 0,r_{1}\mapsto 0,x\mapsto 1,y\mapsto 1\}
,
\gamma=\{r_{0}\mapsto 1,r_{1}\mapsto 1,x\mapsto 1,y\mapsto 1\}
, the latter is a guarded pomset.

Guardedness in pomsets can be characterised by the conjunction of seven properties, which we will discuss now.
On the one hand, these properties have an intuitive explanation as characteristics of behaviours of (possibly concurrent) programs running in isolation.
Hence, if a pomset represents some execution of an isolated program, it is guarded.
On the other hand, the characterisation in terms of these properties provides a proof method to show that a pomset is
not
guarded, by demonstrating the failure of one such property.

We start by observing that guarded pomsets alternate states and actions.
Formally, we can capture this in three properties.
Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]\in\mathsf{SP}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
. We say that
s^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
is a
predecessor
of
s
if it is the latest node ordered strictly before
s
—i.e.,
s

s^{\prime}<_{\mathbf{u}}s
and for all
s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
s^{\prime\prime}<_{\mathbf{u}}s
it holds that
s^{\prime\prime}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}s^{\prime}
.
The notion of
successor
is defined dually.
A node is a
state-node
if it is labelled by an element of
\mathsf{State}
, and an
action-node
otherwise.

(A1)

U
admits a unique minimum and maximum,
*_{\min},*_{\max}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
, labelled by states.

(A2)

Every two related state-nodes are separated by an action-node.

(A3)

Action-nodes have unique state-nodes as neighbours (their predecessor and successor).

The next property formalises the idea
that two related observations cannot contradict each other, such as in the program
(x=1)\cdot(x=2)
. To this end, we need the notion of a
path
. A path for a variable
v
from a state-node
u
to another state-node
s
is a chain such that the changes in the value of
v
between
u
and
s
are explained by the actions between them and recorded in all the states between
u
and
s
.

Definition 5.30

(Path)
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1}),\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
.
We say a
path

p_{v}
from
u_{1}
to
u_{2}
for variable
v\in\textsc{Var}
is a sequence of nodes
q_{1},a_{1},\dots,a_{n},q_{n+1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
that satisfy the following conditions:

(P1)

For all
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(a_{i})\in\mathsf{Act}
and
u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}a_{i}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
for all
i
.
Additionally we require that
a_{i}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}a_{i+1}
for
1\leq i<n
.

(P2)

For all
1\leq i\leq n+1
it holds that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(q_{i})\in\mathsf{State}
, and for all
1\leq i\leq n
, the predecessor of
a_{i}
is
q_{i}
, and the successor of
a_{i}
is
q_{i+1}
.
Additionally we have that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(q_{1})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})
,
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1}))
and

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(q_{n+1})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{2})
.
Lastly, for
1\leq i\leq n
we have:

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(q_{i+1})(v)=\begin{cases}n&\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(a_{i})=v\leftarrow n\text{ for some }n\in\textsc{Val}\\
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(q_{i})(v^{\prime})&\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(a_{i})=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}\text{ for some }v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var},v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(q_{i}))\\
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(q_{i})(v)&\text{otherwise }\end{cases}

Example 5.31
.

The following is a path for
x
:

(x=1)\cdot(y\leftarrow 3)\cdot(x=1)\cdot(x\leftarrow 2)\cdot(x=2\wedge y=3)\cdot(x\leftarrow y)\cdot(x=3)

Note that this is not a path for
y
, because it is not assigned a value by the final atom.

We can now formulate another criterion for a pomset executing in isolation: for every variable in the domain of a state-node, there is a path explaining the changes in value of that variable between the state-node and the maximum node of the pomset.

(A3)

For all state-nodes
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
, there is a path for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
.

Example 5.32
.

The first pomset below satisfies
(A3)
, and the second pomset does not, as there is no path from beginning to end for
x
: the value of
x
in the second observation is not in accordance to the previous assignment.

(x=2\wedge y=2)
(x\leftarrow 4)
(y\leftarrow 3)
(x=4\wedge y=3)

(x=2\wedge y=4)
(x\leftarrow 4)
(x=5\wedge y=4)
(y\leftarrow 2)
(x=4\wedge y=2)

If a pomset represents an isolated program, an action has an effect on its successor.
If that action is of the form
v\leftarrow n
, then the sucessor should assign
n
to
v
; likewise, if the action is of the form
v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
, then the successor should assign the value of
v^{\prime}
to
v
, but the predecessor should also be aware of a value for
v^{\prime}
.

(A4)

If
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
for some
v\in\textsc{Var}
and
n\in\textsc{Val}
, we require that the successor of
u
is
s
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=n
.

(A5)

Let
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
for some
v,v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
and let
p
and
s
be the predecessor, resp. successor, of
u
. Then
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p))
and

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.

Example 5.33
.

The pomset below on the left violates
(A4)
, because the successor of
(x\leftarrow 1)
does not assign
2
to
x
.
On the other hand, the pomset on the right satisfies
(A5)
, because the predecessor of
(x\leftarrow y)
has a value for
y
, and that value is assigned to
x
in the successor.

(x=0)
(x\leftarrow 1)
(x=2)

(x=1\wedge y=2)
(x\leftarrow y)
(x=2\wedge y=2)

Finally, isolated programs cannot observe variables that have not been assigned a value anywhere in the program.
On the pomset-level, this translates to:

(A6)

Let
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be a state-node. Then for all
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
, there exists a path for
v
from
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
to
u

such that either
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s))
and
s=*_{\min}
or
s
is the successor of an assignment-node with label
v\leftarrow k
with
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
.

Example 5.34
.

The pomset on the left does not satisfy
(A6)
, but the one on the right does.

(x=1)
(x\leftarrow 2)
(x=2\wedge y=2)

(x=1)
(y\leftarrow 2)
(x=1\wedge y=2)

Guarded pomsets satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
. In fact, there exists an equivalence:

{thrm}

[]
For
U\in\mathsf{SP}
,
U
is guarded if and only if
U
satisfies
(A1)
–
(A6)
.

Proof 5.35

(Sketch)
.

The forward implication is proved by induction on the construction of
\mathcal{G}
.
For the other direction, we perform induction on the size of
U
(which is possible because
U
is series-parallel and therefore finite).
The induction hypothesis then states that whenever
V
is strictly smaller than
U
, and
V
satisfies the seven properties, then
V
is guarded.
Since
U
satisfies
(A1)
, we know that either it consists of one node labelled by a state, in which case
U
is immediately guarded, or
U=\alpha\cdot V\cdot\beta
for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{State}
and
V\in\mathsf{SP}
.
This gives us four cases to consider:
V=1
,
V=\mathtt{a}
for some
\mathtt{a}\in\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State}
,
V=V_{0}\cdot V_{1}
or
V=V_{0}\parallel V_{1}
.
The first case can be disregarded as
U
would then violate
(A2)
.
In the second case,
(A3)
–
(A6)
can be used to show that
\beta=\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
.
In the latter two cases we show that
U
is built out of two strictly smaller pomsets that satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
, making them guarded by the induction hypothesis.
When these two pomsets are combined to form
U
, this is done according to the rules of guarded pomsets, making
U
guarded as well.

6
Litmus Test

The POCKA-semantics of a program captures all
possible
behaviours
of the program, including all behaviours that could arise when it is put in
parallel with other programs. In this section we look at the behaviour
of the litmus test when it is executed in isolation. In the previous section we saw that if a pomset represents an execution of a program in isolation, it is guarded, and hence it is sufficient to look at the guarded pomsets. We demonstrate that there are in fact no guarded pomsets in the semantics of the litmus test, which shows that it passes. This suggests the guarded fragment of the POCKA-semantics is sequentially consistent: the programs behave as if memory accesses performed concurrently are interleaved and executed sequentially and writes to memory are broadcasted to all threads instantaneously.

Recall the litmus test
t
we considered before, with
\textsc{Var}=\{x,y,r_{0},r_{1}\}
and
\textsc{Val}=\{0,1\}
:

t:=(r_{0}=0\wedge r_{1}=0)\cdot((x\leftarrow 1\cdot r_{0}\leftarrow y)\parallel(y\leftarrow 1\cdot r_{1}\leftarrow x))\cdot\overline{(r_{0}=1\vee r_{1}=1)}

Our strategy for showing that the semantics of
t
does not contain guarded pomsets, is to first show that all pomsets in the semantics of
t
have certain property. We then claim that if a pomset has this property, then it is not guarded, using
(A1)
–
(A6)
from
section

5
.

Definition 6.36

(Litmus Pomsets)
.

Let
x,y,r_{0},r_{1},w\in\textsc{Var}
be distinct and
0,1\in\textsc{Val}
.
A pomset
U=[\mathbf{u}]
has property
P
, denoted
P(U)
, if there exists
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2},w\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
s.t.

1.

the following conditions hold:

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})=(x\leftarrow 1)\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{2})=(y\leftarrow 1)\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(v_{1})=(r_{0}\leftarrow y)\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(v_{2})=(r_{1}\leftarrow x)\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(r_{0})=0=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(r_{1})u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}wu_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w

Graphically, we can represent these conditions as the following diagram:

u_{1}:x\leftarrow 1
u_{2}:y\leftarrow 1
v_{1}:r_{0}\leftarrow x
v_{2}:r_{1}\leftarrow y
w:\left[\begin{array}[]{c@{\mapsto}c}r_{0}&0\\
r_{1}&0\end{array}\right]

2.

For other assignment-nodes in
U
, we have the following conditions. Let
k\in\textsc{Val}\cup\textsc{Var}
.

\forall z.\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(z)=(x\leftarrow k)\Rightarrow z\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}\forall z.\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(z)=(y\leftarrow k)\Rightarrow z\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}\forall z.\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(z)=(r_{0}\leftarrow k)\Rightarrow z\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}\forall z.\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(z)=(r_{1}\leftarrow k)\Rightarrow z\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{2}

The property
P
describes the actions and observations found in the litmus test, and their relative ordering.
For instance,
\forall z.\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(z)=(x\leftarrow n)\Rightarrow z\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
states that all action-nodes that change the value of
x
, occur before node
u_{1}

Hence, the maximal node that alters the value of
x
, changes
x
to
1
. The other requirements are explained similarly.

Lemma 8
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]\in\mathsf{SP}
. If
P(U)
then
U
is not guarded.

We show that
P
is an invariant under closure w.r.t.
\mathsf{exch}
and
\mathsf{contr}
.
To this end, it is useful to study the effect of the contraction order on the level of pomsets; we introduce the following partial order relation on pomsets, analogous to the subsumption order.

Definition 6.37

(Contraction Order)
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]
and
V=[\mathbf{v}]
be pomsets over
\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State}
.
We write
U\preceq V
holds iff there exists a surjection
h\colon S_{\mathbf{v}}\to S_{\mathbf{u}}
satisfying: (i)
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}\circ h=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}
;
(ii)
v\leq_{\mathbf{v}}v^{\prime}
implies
h(v)\leq_{\mathbf{u}}h(v^{\prime})
;
(iii) if
h(v)\leq_{\mathbf{u}}h(v^{\prime})
, then
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v),\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v^{\prime})\in\mathsf{State}
implies
v\leq_{\mathbf{v}}v^{\prime}
or
v^{\prime}\leq_{\mathbf{v}}v
, and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v)
or

\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v^{\prime})\not\in\mathsf{State}
implies
v\leq_{\mathbf{v}}v^{\prime}
.

We then prove the analogue of
lemma

2.10
, relating
\preceq
to closure w.r.t.
\mathsf{contr}
as follows.

Lemma 9
.

Let
L\subseteq\mathsf{SP}
and
U\in\mathsf{SP}
.
Now
U\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
iff
U\preceq V
for some
V\in L
.

With this characterisation in hand, we can prove that
P
is invariant under closure.

Lemma 10
.

Let
e\in\mathcal{T}
. If
\forall U\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
we have
P(U)
, then
\forall V\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
it holds that
P(V)
.

Proof 6.38

(Sketch)
.

By

, Lemma 5.4]
we know that

.

with

and
V\preceq W\sqsubseteq X
.
We then show that
P
is preserved by both of these orders.

Corollary 6.39
.

The semantics of the litmus test contains no guarded pomsets:
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{t}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}\cap\mathcal{G}=\emptyset
.

Proof 6.40
.

All pomsets in
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{t}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
have property
P
if we pick for
u_{1}
the node with label
(x\leftarrow 1)
, for
v_{1}
the node with label
(r_{0}\leftarrow y)
, and same for
u_{2}
and
v_{2}
(see
example

3.20
). Lastly, we pick for
w
the node with label
\delta
.
By
10
we can conclude that all pomsets in
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{t}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
have property
P
, and by
8
we infer that
t
has no guarded pomsets in its semantics.

We showed that we can correctly analyse the litmus test in our algebraic framework. In the next example we show that addition of one extra axiom, which is a commonly made assumption in programming languages, makes the litmus test fail on the guarded semantics.

Example 6.41
.

We add the following axiom, which states that assignments to different variables can be swapped as long as the assigned values are none of the involved variables:

v\leftarrow k\cdot v^{\prime}\leftarrow k^{\prime}\equiv v^{\prime}\leftarrow k^{\prime}\cdot v\leftarrow k\text{ for }v,v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var},\ k,k^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val},\ k^{\prime}\neq v\neq v^{\prime},\ k\neq v\neq v^{\prime}

We show that with this assumption, which is commonly made in programming languages, we get guarded pomsets in the semantics of the litmus program. We can derive:

\displaystyle((r_{0}\leftarrow y)\cdot(r_{1}\leftarrow x))

\displaystyle\equiv((r_{0}\leftarrow y)\parallel 1)\cdot(1\parallel(r_{1}\leftarrow x))

(Unit axiom)

\displaystyle\leqq((r_{0}\leftarrow y)\cdot 1)\parallel(1\cdot(r_{1}\leftarrow x))

(Exchange Law)

\displaystyle\equiv(r_{0}\leftarrow y)\parallel(r_{1}\leftarrow x)

(Unit axiom)

Similarly, we can derive that
(x\leftarrow 1)\cdot(y\leftarrow 1)\leqq(x\leftarrow 1)\parallel(y\leftarrow 1)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle((r_{0}\leftarrow y)\cdot(r_{1}\leftarrow x))\cdot((x\leftarrow 1)\cdot(y\leftarrow 1))\leqq((r_{0}\leftarrow y)\parallel(r_{1}\leftarrow x))\cdot((x\leftarrow 1)\parallel(y\leftarrow 1))

\displaystyle\leqq((r_{0}\leftarrow y)\cdot(x\leftarrow 1))\parallel((r_{1}\leftarrow x)\cdot(y\leftarrow 1))

(Exchange law)

\displaystyle\equiv((x\leftarrow 1)\cdot(r_{0}\leftarrow y))\parallel((y\leftarrow 1)\cdot(r_{1}\leftarrow x))

(New axiom)

Let
e=((r_{0}\leftarrow y)\cdot(r_{1}\leftarrow x))\cdot((x\leftarrow 1)\cdot(y\leftarrow 1))
. We can conclude that

(r_{0}=0\wedge r_{1}=0)\cdot e\cdot\overline{(r_{0}=1\vee r_{1}=1)}\leqq t

From soundness, we infer that
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(r_{0}=0\wedge r_{1}=0)\cdot e\cdot\overline{(r_{0}=1\vee r_{1}=1)}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}\subseteq\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{t}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
. In the left set we find at least one guarded pomset. Let
\alpha=(r_{0}=0\wedge r_{1}=0\wedge x=0\wedge y=0)
,
\beta=(r_{0}=0\wedge r_{1}=0\wedge x=1\wedge y=0)
and
\gamma=(r_{0}=0\wedge r_{1}=0\wedge x=1\wedge y=1)
.

\alpha
(r_{0}\leftarrow y)
\alpha
(r_{1}\leftarrow x)
\alpha
(x\leftarrow 1)
\beta
(y\leftarrow 1)
\gamma

It is easy to show that this pomset is guarded by observing that
\alpha\cdot(r_{0}\leftarrow y)\cdot\alpha
,
\alpha\cdot(r_{1}\leftarrow x)\cdot\alpha
,
\alpha\cdot(x\leftarrow 1)\cdot\beta
and
\beta\cdot(y\leftarrow 1)\cdot\gamma
are all guarded. Hence, by adding this one extra axiom, we find guarded pomsets in the semantics of the litmus test, meaning that this axiom breaks sequential consistency.

7
Discussion

We presented POCKA, a sound and complete algebraic framework that can be used to analyse concurrent programs that manipulate variables. We identified the guarded fragment of the semantics, and showed this fragment captures the behaviour of programs executing in isolation. We demonstrated reasoning in POCKA by analysing a litmus test, also suggesting that the guarded fragment of the POCKA-semantics is sequentially consistent.

This work is built on Kleene algebra and extensions thereof. It is closest to Concurrent Kleene algebra with Observations
[
11
,
12
]
, which was proposed to integrate concurrency with a form of tests (i.e., observations). We deviate from CKAO by using partial observations and accordingly changing the algebraic structure of observations (a PCDL instead of a Boolean algebra), and by incorporating explicit assignments and tests to manipulate variables. Programs such as the litmus test that we analyse in POCKA are outside the scope of CKAO.

The idea of using a PCDL and partial functions in the semantics comes from Jipsen and Moshier
[
10
]
. In the current paper we establish completeness w.r.t. the partial function model, which is missing in
loc. cit.

A further contrast is that POCKA includes as basic syntax atomic programs and assertions pertaining to variable assignment, as occur in the litmus test.
The definition of guarded pomset that we used is close to the one proposed in
[
10
]
. We provided an extensive analysis of guarded pomsets and showed how they can be used to study concrete program behaviour: our new characterisation in terms of concrete properties of pomsets (Theorem
5
) is essential for the analysis of the litmus test in Section
6
.

We suggest three avenues for future research. Firstly, the concrete observations and assignments that we have used are reminiscent of NetKAT
[
2
,
6
]
, an algebraic framework based on Kleene algebra with tests that allows for reasoning about networks. POCKA is thus suggestive of a concurrent version of NetKAT, in which algebraic reasoning about concurrent networks could be studied. While NetKAT arises as a particular instance of KAT, POCKA is
not
an instance of its closest relative in the Kleene algebra family, CKAO, due to the aforementioned move from an
arbitrary
Boolean algebra of observations to a
concrete
PCDL. It would therefore be of interest to formulate the necessary metatheory for the analogous framework of CKA with partial observations (where partial observations are given by an arbitrary PCDL), and situate POCKA within it.

This naturally leads to a third line of research. We have used the CKAH framework to obtain a completeness proof, and it turned out that the proof technique was perfectly amenable to a replacement of the Boolean algebra structure of observations with our observation algebra. This raises the question: which conditions are necessary on the algebraic structure of observations to be able to prove completeness in a similar manner? In particular, what conditions are needed for a result similar to
lemma

4.21
to hold? Our conjecture is that the observation algebra needs to be such that all elements can be written as a finite sum of join-irreducible elements of the algebra (cf.
4
).

Appendix A
Proofs about observation algebra

See
2

Proof A.42
.

The fact that
\mathrm{OA}
is a PCDL and the assignment
{\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket}
is well-defined establishes the soundness of the PCDL axioms. We thus only have the domain-specific axioms left to verify.

•

If
n\neq m
, it is immediate that

{\left\llbracket v=n\wedge v=m\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket v=n\right\rrbracket}\cap{\left\llbracket v=m\right\rrbracket}=\{\alpha\;\;|\;\;\alpha(v)=n\}\cap\{\alpha\;\;|\;\;\alpha(v)=m\}=\emptyset={\left\llbracket\bot\right\rrbracket}

•

Next we show
{\left\llbracket\overline{v=n}\right\rrbracket}\subseteq{\left\llbracket\bigvee_{m\neq n}v=m\right\rrbracket}
.
Assume

\alpha\in{\left\llbracket\overline{v=n}\right\rrbracket}=\bigcup\{B\in P_{\leq}(\mathsf{State})\mid B\cap{\left\llbracket v=n\right\rrbracket}=\emptyset\}.

We have some downwards-closed
B\subseteq\mathsf{State}
such that
\alpha\in B
and
B\cap{\left\llbracket v=n\right\rrbracket}=\emptyset
.
Suppose towards a contradiction that
\alpha(v)
is undefined, or that
\alpha(v)=n
.
We can then choose
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{State}
to be
n
on
v
, and identical to
\alpha
elsewhere; in that case,
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
, which means that
\alpha^{\prime}\in B
.
But then, since
\alpha^{\prime}\in{\left\llbracket v=n\right\rrbracket}
by construction, we have a contradiction with the fact that
B\cap{\left\llbracket v=n\right\rrbracket}=\emptyset
.
Thus, there exists an
m\in\textsc{Val}
such that
\alpha(v)=m
and
m\neq n
.
It then follows that
\alpha\in{\left\llbracket\bigvee_{m\neq n}v=m\right\rrbracket}
.

•

Next we prove that
{\left\llbracket\overline{\bigwedge_{i}v_{i}=n_{i}}\right\rrbracket}\subseteq{\left\llbracket\bigvee_{i}\overline{v_{i}=n_{i}}\right\rrbracket}
, if the
v_{i}
are distinct.
Let
\alpha\in B
such that
B\cap{\left\llbracket\bigwedge_{i}v_{i}=n_{i}\right\rrbracket}=\emptyset
.
We claim that for some
i
,
\alpha(v_{i})=m\neq n_{i}
. Suppose otherwise: then for each
v_{i}
either
\alpha(v_{i})=n_{i}
or
\alpha(v_{i})
is undefined. Define:

\alpha^{\prime}(v)::=\begin{cases}n_{i}&\text{ if }v=v_{i}\text{ and }\alpha(v_{i})\text{ undefined;}\\
\alpha(v)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

This is well-defined by the assumption that the
v_{i}
are all distinct. By construction,
\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha
and

\alpha^{\prime}\in\bigcap_{i}\{\beta\;\;|\;\;\beta(v_{i})=n_{i}\}={\left\llbracket\bigwedge_{i}v_{i}=n_{i}\right\rrbracket}
. As
B
is downwards-closed, we get
\alpha^{\prime}\in B
, contradicting
B\cap{\left\llbracket\bigwedge_{i}v_{i}=n_{i}\right\rrbracket}=\emptyset
. Hence for some
i
,
\alpha(v_{i})=m\neq n_{i}
. Hence
\alpha\in{\left\llbracket\overline{v_{i}=n_{i}}\right\rrbracket}\subseteq{\left\llbracket\bigvee_{i}\overline{v_{i}=n_{i}}\right\rrbracket}
as required.

For the inductive step, we verify that the closure rules for congruence preserve soundness. This is all immediate from the definition of
{\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket}
. For instance, if
e=e_{0}\lor e_{1}
,
f=f_{0}\lor f_{1}
,
e_{0}\equiv f_{0}
and
e_{1}\equiv f_{1}
, then
{\left\llbracket e\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket e_{0}\right\rrbracket}\cup{\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket f_{0}\right\rrbracket}\cup{\left\llbracket f_{1}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket f\right\rrbracket}
,
where we have used that
{\left\llbracket e_{0}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket f_{0}\right\rrbracket}
and
{\left\llbracket e_{1}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket f_{1}\right\rrbracket}
by the induction hypothesis.

See
3

Proof A.43
.

Assume
\alpha\in{\left\llbracket\pi_{\beta}\right\rrbracket}
. Then for all
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\beta)
,
\alpha(v)
is defined and
\alpha(v)=\beta(v)
, hence
\alpha\leq\beta
. Assume
\alpha\leq\beta
. Then

\pi_{\alpha}\leqq\pi_{\beta}
is established from
\pi_{\alpha}\wedge\pi_{\beta}\equiv\pi_{\alpha}
: by the assumption, every conjunct in
\bigwedge_{\beta(v)=n}v=n
appears as a conjunct in
\bigwedge_{\alpha(v)=n}v=n
, so by idempotence
\pi_{\alpha}\land\pi_{\beta}\equiv\Bigl(\bigwedge_{\alpha(v)=n}v=n\Bigr)\wedge\Bigl(\bigwedge_{\beta(v)=n}v=n\Bigr)\equiv\bigwedge_{\alpha(v)=n}v=n\equiv\pi_{\alpha}
.
Finally, assume
\pi_{\alpha}\leqq\pi_{\beta}
. By soundness
{\left\llbracket\pi_{\alpha}\right\rrbracket}\subseteq{\left\llbracket\pi_{\beta}\right\rrbracket}
, and it is trivial to
establish
\alpha\in{\left\llbracket\pi_{\alpha}\right\rrbracket}
.

See
4

Proof A.44
.

Noting that
\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq p\}\leqq p
by definition, we focus on the other inequality,
proceeding by induction on
p
. For the base cases,
\bot\leqq\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq\bot\}
by definition;
\top\leqq\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq\top\}
as

\top\equiv\pi_{\emptyset}\in\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq\top\}
; and

v=n\leqq\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq v=n\}
as

v=n\equiv\pi_{\{v\mapsto n\}}\in\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq v=n\}
.

In the induction step we have three cases.

•

If
p=p_{0}\wedge p_{1}
, by the inductive hypothesis and distributivity we obtain

p_{0}\land p_{1}\leqq\bigvee\{\alpha\land\beta\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0},\beta\leqq p_{1}\}.

We claim that

\{\alpha\land\beta\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0},\beta\leqq p_{1}\}\subseteq\{\alpha\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\land p_{1}\}\cup\{\bot\}
. Call
\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{State}

compatible
if,
for any
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)\cap\mathsf{dom}(\beta)
,
\alpha(v)=\beta(v)
. Take
\alpha\leqq p_{0}
and

\beta\leqq p_{1}
. There are two cases. In the first,
\alpha
and
\beta
are compatible. Then
define
\gamma
by

\gamma(v)::=\begin{cases}\alpha(v)&\text{ if }\alpha(v)\text{ defined;}\\
\beta(v)&\text{ if }\beta(v)\text{ defined;}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{ otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

This is well-defined by compatability. Then
\gamma\leq\alpha
as well as
\gamma\leq\beta
, and hence by
3
we find
\gamma\leqq\alpha\land\beta\leqq p_{0}\land p_{1}
.
In the other case,
\alpha
and
\beta
are not compatible: hence for some distinct
n
and
m
,
v=n
and
v=m
are among the conjuncts of
\alpha\land\beta
.
By the axiom
v=n\land v=m\equiv\bot
, it then follows that
\alpha\land\beta\equiv\bot
.
We obtain

\bigvee\{\alpha\land\beta\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0},\beta\leqq p_{1}\}\leqq\bigvee(\{\alpha\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\land p_{1}\}\cup\{\bot\})\equiv\bigvee\{\alpha\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\land p_{1}\}.

•

If
p=p_{0}\vee p_{1}
, we derive

\displaystyle p_{0}\vee p_{1}

\displaystyle\leqq\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}\vee\bigvee\{\beta\in\mathsf{State}\mid\beta\leqq p_{1}\}

(IH)

\displaystyle\leqq\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq p_{1}\vee p_{2}\}

(
\alpha\leqq p_{0}\leqq p_{0}\vee p_{1}
, similar for
\beta
)

•

If
p=\overline{p_{0}}
, we derive

\displaystyle\overline{p_{0}}

\displaystyle\equiv\overline{\bigvee\{\alpha\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}}

(IH)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge\{\overline{\alpha}\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}

(De Morgan)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigwedge\{\overline{\bigwedge_{\alpha(v)=n}v=n}\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}

(Definition of
\pi_{\alpha}=\alpha
)

\displaystyle\leqq\bigwedge\{\bigvee_{\alpha(v)=n}\overline{v=n}\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}

(De Morgan-like domain-specific axiom)

\displaystyle\leqq\bigwedge\{\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha(v)=n\\
m\neq n\end{subarray}}v=m\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}

(Pseudocomplement domain-specific axiom)

Note that the De Morgan law applied in the second step is indeed satisfied by PCDLs
[
3
]
.
Now, define
K::=\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}
,
J_{\alpha}::=\{(v,m)\mid\alpha(v)=n\neq m\}
,
J::=\bigcup_{\alpha\in K}J_{\alpha}
and
F::=\{f:K\rightarrow J\mid\forall\alpha\in K,f(\alpha)\in J_{\alpha}\}
. Further, let

p_{\alpha,(v,m)}::=\begin{cases}v=m&\text{ if }\alpha(v)=n\neq m;\\
\bot&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Then

\bigwedge\{\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha(v)=n\\
m\neq n\end{subarray}}v=m\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}\equiv\bigwedge_{\alpha\in K}\bigvee_{(v,m)\in J_{\alpha}}p_{\alpha,(v,m)}\equiv\bigvee_{f\in F}\bigwedge_{\alpha\in K}p_{\alpha,f(\alpha)}

by distributivity. For each
f\in F
, if the
p_{\alpha,f(\alpha)}
are compatible,

\bigwedge_{\alpha\in K}p_{\alpha,f(\alpha)}\equiv\beta_{f}
, for a
\beta_{f}
with the property that for every

\alpha\leqq p_{0}
,
\beta_{f}\land\alpha=\bot
(as by definition, for each such
\alpha
,
\beta_{f}

has some
v=m
as a conjunct, where
\alpha
has a conjunct
v=n
for
n\neq m
). If they are incompatible,

\bigwedge_{\alpha\in K}p_{\alpha,f(\alpha)}\equiv\bot
. Hence

\bigvee_{f\in F}\bigwedge_{\alpha\in K}p_{\alpha,f(\alpha)}\leqq\bigvee\{\beta\mid\text{for all }\alpha,\alpha\leqq p_{0}\text{ implies }\alpha\land\beta\equiv\bot\}.

For any
\beta
satisfying the property that
\text{for all }\alpha,\alpha\leqq p_{0}\text{ implies }\alpha\land\beta\equiv\bot
, we have

\beta\land p_{0}\equiv\beta\land\bigvee\{\alpha\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}\equiv\bigvee\{\alpha\land\beta\mid\alpha\leqq p_{0}\}\equiv\bot
, so
\beta\leqq\overline{p_{0}}
and

\bigvee\{\beta\mid\text{for all }\alpha\leqq p_{0}\text{ implies }\alpha\land\beta\equiv\bot\}\leqq\bigvee\{\beta\mid\beta\leqq\overline{p_{0}}\},

completing the proof.

See
3.1

Proof A.45
.

The left-to-right direction follows from
2
.
For the right-to-left direction, suppose that
{\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket}
. By
4
, we obtain that

{\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq p\}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket\bigvee\{\beta\in\mathsf{State}\mid\beta\leqq q\}\right\rrbracket}={\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket}.

We prove
\alpha\leqq p
if and only if
\alpha\leqq q
. Take
\alpha\leqq p
.
Then
\alpha\in{\left\llbracket\alpha\right\rrbracket}\subseteq{\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket}=\bigcup_{\beta\leqq q}{\left\llbracket\beta\right\rrbracket}
by
2
and
3
.
Hence for some
\beta\leqq q
,
\alpha\in{\left\llbracket\beta\right\rrbracket}
, and by
3
once more,

\alpha\leqq\beta\leqq q
. The other direction is symmetric. It follows that

p\equiv\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq p\}\equiv\bigvee\{\alpha\in\mathsf{State}\mid\alpha\leqq q\}\equiv q,

as required.

Appendix B
Proofs towards completeness

The following three results are all needed in the proofs that follow, and come from
[
12
]
.
First of all, we can prove the following useful properties about the interaction between closure and other operators on pomset languages:

Lemma B.46
.

Let
L,K\subseteq\mathsf{Pom}
and
C\in\mathsf{PC}
.
The following hold.

1.

L\subseteq{{K}{\downarrow^{H}}}
iff
{{L}{\downarrow^{H}}}\subseteq{{K}{\downarrow^{H}}}
.

2.

If
L\subseteq K
, then
{{L}{\downarrow^{H}}}\subseteq{{K}{\downarrow^{H}}}
.

3.

{\left(L\cup K\right){\downarrow^{H}}}={\left({{L}{\downarrow^{H}}}\cup{{K}{\downarrow^{H}}}\right){\downarrow^{H}}}

4.

{\left(L\cdot K\right){\downarrow^{H}}}={\left({{L}{\downarrow^{H}}}\cdot{{K}{\downarrow^{H}}}\right){\downarrow^{H}}}

5.

{\left(L\parallel K\right){\downarrow^{H}}}={\left({{L}{\downarrow^{H}}}\parallel{{K}{\downarrow^{H}}}\right){\downarrow^{H}}}

6.

{\left(L^{*}\right){\downarrow^{H}}}={({\left({L}{\downarrow^{H}}\right)}^{*})}{\downarrow^{H}}

7.

If
{L}{\downarrow^{H}}\subseteq{K}{\downarrow^{H}}
, then
{C[L]}{\downarrow^{H}}\subseteq{C[K]}{\downarrow^{H}}
.

8.

If
L\subseteq\mathsf{SP}
, then
{L}{\downarrow^{H}}\subseteq\mathsf{SP}
.

Second, we can note the following result about the interaction between
\mathsf{exch}
and
\mathsf{contr}
:

Lemma B.47
.

For any
L\in 2^{\mathsf{SP}}
, we have
{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}\cup\mathsf{exch}}}={({L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}}})}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.

We can then prove soundness of the POCKA semantics w.r.t. the axioms.

See
3.2

Proof B.48
.

By construction it is immediate that
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{-}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
is closed under closure with respect to
\mathsf{exch}\cup\mathsf{contr}
.
We then proceed by induction on
\equiv
. For all the pairs from
\equiv_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{BKA}}
, it follows from
theorem

6
that
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{f}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
. Then immediately their POCKA-semantics also coincide. For all
the pairs from
\equiv_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}
,
we make use of
section

3.1
. Note that
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{-}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
almost coincides with
{\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}
on
\mathcal{O}
, so the proof is very straightforward.
For instance, take
p\vee(p\wedge q)\equiv_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}p
. Then
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\vee(p\wedge q)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}=\mathsf{State}^{*}\cdot{\left\llbracket p\vee(p\wedge q)\right\rrbracket}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}\cdot\mathsf{State}^{*}
.
From
section

3.1
, we know that
{\left\llbracket p\vee(p\wedge q)\right\rrbracket}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}={\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}
, and thus we obtain

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\vee(p\wedge q)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}=\mathsf{State}^{*}\cdot{\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}\cdot\mathsf{State}^{*}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
. Then from this we can conclude that

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\vee(p\wedge q)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\vee(p\wedge q)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}\cup\mathsf{contr}}}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}\cup\mathsf{contr}}}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
.
We can prove soundness of the other observation algebra axioms analogously.

The next axiom is the exchange law. We show that

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\parallel f)\cdot(g\parallel h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}\subseteq\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\cdot g)\parallel(f\cdot h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
.
By
lemma

B.46
(
1
), it
suffices to prove that

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\parallel f)\cdot(g\parallel h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\cdot g)\parallel(f\cdot h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
. Take an element in
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\parallel f)\cdot(g\parallel h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
. This is thus a pomset of the form
(X\parallel Y)\cdot(V\parallel W)
for
X\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
,
Y\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{f}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
,

V\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{g}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
and
W\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{h}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
. Thus we immediately obtain that

(X\cdot V)\parallel(Y\cdot W)\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\cdot g)\parallel(f\cdot h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
. From
lemma

B.47
, we know that

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{-}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}={({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{-}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}}})}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
. We know that
(X\parallel Y)\cdot(V\parallel W)\sqsubseteq(X\cdot V)\parallel(Y\cdot W)
and that
(X\cdot V)\parallel(Y\cdot W)\in{({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\cdot g)\parallel(f\cdot h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}}})}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.
Then we can apply
lemmas

B.47
and
2.10
to obtain that
(X\parallel Y)\cdot(V\parallel W)\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{(e\cdot g)\parallel(f\cdot h)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{491520}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{344064}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-2.5pt}{\resizebox{245760}{655360}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
.

Left to verify are the interface axioms.
To check
p\wedge q\leqq p\cdot q
it again suffices to prove that

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\wedge q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\cdot q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
by

lemma

B.46
(
1
). We take an element in

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\wedge q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
. This a pomset of the form
U\cdot\alpha\cdot V
such
that
U,V\in\mathsf{State}^{*}
and
\alpha\in{\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}\cap{\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{OA}}
. We can establish that
U\cdot\alpha\cdot\alpha\cdot V\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\cdot q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
. Now take the pomset context
C=U\cdot*\cdot V
.
We have that
C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\cdot\alpha\right\rrbracket}]=\{U\cdot\alpha\cdot\alpha\cdot V\}\subseteq\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\cdot q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}\subseteq\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{p\cdot q}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
. Then by closure we find

.

Since
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{\bot}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}=\emptyset=\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
, it follows that
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{\bot}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}=\emptyset=\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{0}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
.

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}
