Proof B.57
.

If
e\leq f\in\mathsf{contr}
, then
e=\alpha
and
f=\alpha\cdot\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
.
We can then derive that
\alpha\equiv\alpha\wedge\alpha\leqq\alpha\cdot\alpha
, using the PCDL and the Interface axioms. Hence
e\leqq f
.
If
e\leq f\in\mathsf{top}
, then we distinguish two cases.

1.

Let
e=\alpha\cdot c
and
g=c
for
c\in\mathsf{Act}
and
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
.
Then we derive

\alpha\cdot c\leqq\top\cdot c\leqq c

The case where
e=c\cdot\alpha
is similar.

2.

Let
e=\alpha\cdot\beta
and
g=\beta
for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{State}
.
Then we derive

\alpha\cdot\beta\leqq\top\cdot\beta\leqq\beta

The case where
e=\beta\cdot\alpha
is similar. Hence, we can conclude that
e\leqq f
.

Appendix C
Proofs about guardedness

We can establish the following three facts about paths.

Lemma C.58
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
.
If
p_{v}
is a path from
u_{1}
to
u_{2}
for
v\in\textsc{Var}
, then for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in{q_{1},\dots,q_{n+1}}
such that
z_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}z_{2}
, the action-nodes and state-nodes between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
in
p_{v}
form a path from
z_{1}
to
z_{2}
for
v
.

Proof C.59
.

Let
a_{k},\dots,a_{m}
be the action-nodes between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
on
p_{v}
. We know by construction that
z_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}a_{k}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}a_{k+1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}\cdots\leq_{\mathbf{u}}a_{m}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}z_{2}
. This verifies the first property of a path.
For the state-nodes we take the nodes
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
and the state-nodes between them in
p_{v}
. We denote these state-nodes with
q_{k},\cdots q_{m+1}
. They satisfy
(P2)
by construction.

The following notion is also useful.

Definition C.60

(Bottleneck)
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
and
u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
.
We say
u_{1}
is a
bottleneck between
u_{0}
and
u_{2}
if
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
and
for all
u_{3}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
s.t.
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{3}
we have
u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{3}
or
u_{3}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.

Lemma C.61
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
s.t.
u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
.
If there exists a path
p_{v}
from
u_{1}
to
u_{2}
, and a bottleneck
u_{3}
between them, then the bottleneck is on
p_{v}
.

Proof C.62
.

Proof by contradiction. Suppose that
u_{3}
is not on
p_{v}
. Take the biggest element of the path that is below
u_{3}
, denote it with
s_{1}
. If
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s_{1})\in\mathsf{Act}
, we know it has a unique successor state-node that is on
p_{v}
, denote that with
s_{2}
.
This means that
u_{3}<_{\mathbf{u}}s_{2}
as
s_{1}
was the biggest element below
u_{3}
on
p_{v}
. This is a contradiction with the fact that
s_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{3}
and
u_{3}
not on
p_{v}
. Now suppose that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
.
If
u_{2}=s_{1}
, then we get that
u_{3}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{3}
, which is a contradiction. Thus we know that on
p_{v}
there must be a node with an action label after
s_{1}
, let us call it
s_{3}
and its unique predecessor is
s_{1}
.
And thus we have
u_{3}<_{\mathbf{u}}s_{3}
. From this we can conclude that
u_{3}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}s_{1}
which can only be true if
u_{3}=s_{1}
, which is a contradiction.

Lemma C.63
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
and
u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
.
If there exists a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
u_{0}
to
u_{1}
, and a path
s_{v}
for
v
from
u_{1}
to
u_{2}
, then the path
t_{v}
obtained by taking the union of
p_{v}
and
s_{v}
is a path for
v
from
u_{0}
to
u_{2}
.

Proof C.64
.

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
be the action-nodes in
p_{v}
and

b_{1},\dots,b_{m}
the action nodes of
s_{v}
. The action-nodes of

t_{v}
are then
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},b_{1},\dots,b_{m}=c_{1},\dots,c_{n+m}
. The first requirement of a path is then automatically
satisfied for
t_{v}
, by construction of
\leq_{\mathbf{u}}
. Take

q_{1},\dots,q_{n+1}
to be the state-nodes in
p_{v}
and

w_{1},\dots,w_{m+1}
the state-nodes in
s_{v}
and
let
q_{1},\dots,q_{n+1},w_{2},\dots,w_{m+1}
be the state-nodes of
t_{v}
, which we will denote with
x_{1},\dots,x_{n+m+1}
. Per definition we know that
q_{n+1}=w_{1}
.
We need that the predecessor of
c_{i}
is
x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n+m
. If
1\leq i\leq n
, this condition is immediately satisfied. For
n+1<i\leq n+m
, we know that
c_{i}
is
b_{j}
for some
1\leq j\leq m
. Thus
i=j+n
.
The predecessor of
b_{j}
is
w_{j}
, which is
x_{j+n}=x_{i}
. For the case where
i=n+1
, we know that the predecessor of
c_{n+1}
is the predecessor of
b_{1}
, which is
w_{1}=q_{n+1}
. We have that
x_{i}=x_{n+1}
, so we have obtained the desired result. We can give a similar argument why the successor of
c_{i}
is
x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n+m
. The other properties of a path can be verified in a similar manner.

We introduce the following definition in order to aid in the proof of why
(A1)
–
(A6)
hold in a pomset if and only if that pomset is
guarded
.

Definition C.65
.

Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]
and
V=[\mathbf{v}]
be pomsets.
We say that
U
is a
convex subpomset
of
V
when there exists an injective function
h\colon S_{\mathbf{u}}\to S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that all of the following hold:

(i)

Labels are partially preserved. We have for
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
, if
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{Act}
or
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(h(u))\in\mathsf{Act}
then
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}\circ h(u)=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}
, and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
if and only if
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}\circ h(u)\in\mathsf{State}
.

(ii)

Order is preserved and reflected, i.e., for
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
we have
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
if and only if
h(u_{0})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{1})
.

(iii)

h
is convex, i.e., if
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
and
v\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
with
h(u_{0})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}v\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{1})
, then
h(u)=v
for some
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
.

Lemma C.66
.

Let
U,V\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
and
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
. Assume
U
is a convex subpomset of
V
such that
h
maps the minimum of
U
to the minimum of
V
and same for the maximum. If
u_{1}
is the successor or predecessor of
u_{0}
, then
h(u_{1})
is the successor, resp. predecessor, of
h(u_{0})
in
V
.

Proof C.67
.

If
u_{1}
is the successor of
u_{0}
in
U
, we know that
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
. By convexity, we obtain
h(u_{0})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{1})
. Now suppose that there exists
u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
h(u_{0})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}u_{2}
.
As we know that
h(*_{\max})
is the maximum of
V
, we know that
h(u_{0})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}u_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(*_{\max})
. By convexity, we obtain
u_{2}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
h(u_{2}^{\prime})=u_{2}
and
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}^{\prime}
.
As
u_{1}
is the successor of
u_{0}
, we get that
u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}^{\prime}
.
By convexity, this means that
h(u_{1})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{2}^{\prime})
. As
h(u_{2}^{\prime})=u_{2}
, we have obtained the desired result.
The case where
u_{1}
is the predecessor of
u_{0}
in
U
is proved analogously.

Lemma C.68
.

Let
U,V\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
s.t.
U
is a convex subpomset of
V
. If
h(u_{1})
is the successor, resp. predecessor, of
h(u_{0})
in
V
, then
u_{1}
is the successor, resp. predecessor, of
u_{0}
in
U
.

Proof C.69
.

If
h(u_{1})
is the successor of
h(u_{0})
in
V
, we know that
h(u_{0})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{1})
. Then, by convexity, we obtain
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
. Now suppose that there exists
u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u_{0}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
.
Thus we know that
h(u_{0})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{2})
. As
h(u_{1})
is the successor of
h(u_{0})
, we obtain
h(u_{1})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{2})
and subsequently that
u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
.
For the case where
h(u_{1})
is the predecessor of
h(u_{0})
in
V
, the proof is analogous.

Lemma C.70
.

Let
U,V\in\mathsf{Pom}(\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State})
.
If
U
is a convex subpomset of
V
with a unique state-labelled minimum and maximum, and
V
obeys
(A1)
–
(A3)
, then so does
U
.

Proof C.71
.

Since
U
has a unique state-labelled minimum and maximum, it suffices to verify
(A2)
and
(A3)
.
Let
U=[\mathbf{u}]
and
V=[\mathbf{v}]
, and let
U
be a convex subpomset of
V
with
h\colon S_{\mathbf{u}}\to S_{\mathbf{v}}
.

(A2)

Suppose
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{0}),\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
; in that case, we know that
h(u_{0})<_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{0})
, since
h
is order-preserving and injective.
Since
V
is has the property of
(A2)
, we obtain
v\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
h(u_{0})<_{\mathbf{v}}v<_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{1})
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v)\in\mathsf{Act}
.
Because
h
is convex, we obtain
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
h(u)=v
, which tells us that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}u<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.
Since
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(h(u))=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v)\in\mathsf{Act}
, we get
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{Act}
, and the condition is satisfied.

(A3)

Suppose
u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
with
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{0})\in\mathsf{Act}
; hence, we know that
h(u_{0})\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
with
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(h(u_{0}))\in\mathsf{Act}
.
Since
V
obeys
(A3)
, we obtain
v\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v)\in\mathsf{State}
, and
v
is the predecessor of
h(u_{0})
in
S_{\mathbf{v}}
.
Let
*_{\min}
be the unique state-labelled
\leq_{\mathbf{u}}
-minimum of
S_{\mathbf{u}}
, which exists by the premise.
We then know that
*_{\min}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{0}
, and hence
h(*_{\min})<_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{0})
.
Since
v
is the predecessor of
h(u_{0})
, it follows that
h(*_{\min})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}v\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{0})
; by convexity of
h
we obtain
u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
h(u_{1})=v
.
It is easily seen that
u_{1}
is state-labelled.
To see that
u_{1}
is the predecessor of
u_{0}
in
S_{\mathbf{u}}
, first note that since
h(u_{1})<_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{0})
and
h
is order-reflecting, it follows that
u_{1}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{0}
.
Moreover, if
u_{1}^{\prime}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{0}
, then
h(u_{1}^{\prime})<_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{0})
, and hence
h(u_{1}^{\prime})\leq_{\mathbf{v}}h(u_{1})
, meaning
u_{1}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.

See
5

Proof C.72
.

We first prove the implication from left to right.
Note that guarded pomsets are series-parallel by construction.
To show that guarded pomsets satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
, we proceed by induction on the construction of
\mathcal{G}
.
In the base, where
U=\alpha
for
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
or
U=\alpha\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
for
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
and
\mathtt{a}\in\mathsf{Act}
, the requirements hold immediately.
In the inductive step, there are two cases, and for each of these cases we need to verify all seven properties.

•

If
U=V\cdot\alpha\cdot W
such that
[\mathbf{v}]=V\cdot\alpha
and
[\mathbf{w}]=\alpha\cdot W
are guarded, then
V\cdot\alpha
and
\alpha\cdot W
satisfy all seven properties by induction.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
S_{\mathbf{u}}=S_{\mathbf{v}}\cup S_{\mathbf{w}}
and

{\leq_{\mathbf{u}}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v}}}\cup{\leq_{\mathbf{w}}}\cup S_{\mathbf{v}}\times S_{\mathbf{w}}
and

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}\cup\lambda_{\mathbf{w}}
, with
S_{\mathbf{v}}\cap S_{\mathbf{w}}=\{*\}
such that
*
is the node labelled by
\alpha
in
U
.
In particular, this means that for all
v\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
and
w\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
we have that
v\leq_{\mathbf{v}}*\leq_{\mathbf{w}}w
.

(A1)

The minimum node of
[\mathbf{v}]
is also the minimum of
[\mathbf{u}]
, and similarly the maximum node of
[\mathbf{w}]
is the maximum node of
[\mathbf{u}]
; since these nodes are state-labelled by induction, the condition holds.

(A2)

Let
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{0}),\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
and
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.
If
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
, then
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{1}
and (since
[\mathbf{v}]
satisfies
(A2)
) we find
u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{2}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{1}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(u_{2})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
From this, it follows that
u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{2})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
The case where
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
is similar.

We are left with the case where
u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\setminus\{*\}
and
u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{w}}\setminus\{*\}
.
Since
[\mathbf{v}]
satisfies
(A2)
and
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{v}}*
, we obtain
u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{2}<_{\mathbf{v}}*
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(u_{2})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
Because
*<_{\mathbf{w}}u_{1}
, it then follows that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}<_{\mathbf{u}}*<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{2})\in\mathsf{Act}
.

(A3)

Let
u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{0})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
If
u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
, then by induction there exists a
u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
and
u_{1}
is the predecessor of
u_{0}
in
[\mathbf{v}]
.
Since
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
, it remains to prove that
u_{1}
is the predecessor of
u_{0}
in
[\mathbf{u}]
as well. To see this, note that
u_{1}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{0}
, and if
u_{1}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u_{1}^{\prime}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{0}
, then
u_{1}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
by
definition of
[\mathbf{u}]
, and hence also
u_{1}^{\prime}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{0}
,
meaning that
u_{1}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathbf{v}}u_{1}
, and thus
u_{1}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.
To show that
u_{0}
has a state-node as successor as well, we can argue similarly.
The case where
u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
is analogous.

(A4)

If we take
u\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
, we know immediately that for every
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
there is a path from
u
to
*_{\max}
by the induction hypothesis, where
*_{\max}
is the maximum node of
[\mathbf{u}]
. Thus we only consider the case where
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\setminus\{*\}
.
From the induction hypothesis we obtain a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
u
to
*
in
V
, and a path
q_{v}
for
v
from
*
to
*_{\max}
in
W
.
Note that
*_{\max}
in
W
is the same node as
*_{\max}
in
U
. Using
lemma

C.63
, we obtain a path for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
.

(A5)

This item follows immediately from the induction hypothesis.

(A6)

This item also follows immediately from the induction hypothesis.

(A7)

If we take
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
, we know immediately that the condition is satisfied by the induction hypothesis. Thus we only consider the case where
u\in S_{\mathbf{w}}\setminus\{*\}
. Take
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
.
From the induction hypothesis we obtain a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
s_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
to
u
in
[\mathbf{w}]
such that
s_{0}=*
, or
s_{0}
is the successor of an assignment-node with label
v\leftarrow k
with
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
. In the latter case, we are done immediately. In the case where
s_{0}=*
, we know this means that
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(*))
.
From our induction hypothesis we then obtain a path
q_{v}
from
s_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
s_{1}=*_{\min}
, or
s_{1}
is the successor of an assignment-node with label
v\leftarrow k
with
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
. The union of
q_{v}
and
p_{v}
is then a path for
v
from
s_{1}
to
u
in
U
such that
s_{1}=*_{\min}
, or
s_{1}
is
the successor of an assignment-node with label
v\leftarrow k
with
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
(
lemma

C.63
).

•

If
U=\alpha\oplus\gamma\cdot(V\parallel W)\cdot\beta\oplus\delta
such that
[\mathbf{v}]=\alpha\cdot V\cdot\beta
and
[\mathbf{w}]=\gamma\cdot W\cdot\delta
are guarded, then
[\mathbf{v}]

and
[\mathbf{w}]
satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
by induction.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
S_{\mathbf{u}}=S_{\mathbf{v}}\cup S_{\mathbf{w}}
and
{\leq_{\mathbf{u}}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v}}}\cup{\leq_{\mathbf{w}}}
,
with
S_{\mathbf{v}}\cap S_{\mathbf{w}}=\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
, with
*_{\min}
the node with label

\alpha
in
[\mathbf{v}]
and label
\gamma
in
[\mathbf{w}]
and
*_{\max}
the node with label
\beta
in

[\mathbf{v}]
and label
\delta
in
[\mathbf{w}]
. We also have
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\min})=\alpha\oplus\gamma

and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\max})=\beta\oplus\delta
and for all nodes
x\in S_{\mathbf{u}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
have
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(x)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(x)

if
x\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(x)
if
x\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
.

(A1)

To see that
*_{\min}
is the minimum of
[\mathbf{u}]
, note that if
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
then either
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
or
u\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
.
In the former case, we know that
*_{\min}\leq_{\mathbf{v}}s
, and hence
*_{\min}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}s
.
The case where
u\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
can be argued similarly.
We can show that
*_{\max}
is the maximum of
[\mathbf{u}]
analogously.
Lastly, we note that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\min}),\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\max})\in\mathsf{State}
.

(A2)

Let
u_{0},u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{0}),\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
and
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.
By definition of
\leq_{\mathbf{u}}
, we have that either
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{1}
or
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{w}}u_{1}
. W.l.o.g. we assume
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{1}
. We obtain
u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{2}<_{\mathbf{v}}u_{1}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(u_{2})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
It then follows that
u_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}<_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{2})\in\mathsf{Act}
.

(A3)

Let
u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{0})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
W.l.o.g. let
u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
. Hence, we find
u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
and
u_{1}
is the predecessor of
u_{0}
in
[\mathbf{v}]
from the induction hypothesis.
Since
u_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
automatically, it remains to show that
u_{1}
is the predecessor of
u_{0}
in
[\mathbf{u}]
as well. This follows immediately from
lemma

C.66
. That
u_{0}
has a state-node as its successor is proved analogously.

(A4)

Let
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
. Take
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
. W.l.o.g. assume that
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
. If
u\neq*_{\min}
and
u\neq*_{\max}
,
then we
find a path for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
in
[\mathbf{v}]
. This immediately is also a path for
v
from
u_{0}
to
*_{\max}
in
[\mathbf{u}]
as
\beta\subseteq\beta\oplus\delta
.
If
u=*_{\max}
, there is a trivial path for
v
from
*_{\max}
to
*_{\max}
. If
u=*_{\min}
, we can assume without loss of generality that
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
, and use our induction hypothesis to obtain a path for
v
in a way similar to the first case.

(A5)

Let
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
for some
v\in\textsc{Var}
and
n\in\textsc{Val}
. W.l.o.g. we assume
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
. We find
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such
that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(s)\in\mathsf{State}
,
s
is the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}]
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(s)(v)=n
. We immediately obtain
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=n
.
By
lemma

C.66
, using the identity function as witness to show that
[\mathbf{v}]
is a convex subpomset of
[\mathbf{u}]
, we get that
s
is the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{u}]
, and we are done.

(A6)

Let
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
for some
v,v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
. If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
, then we find
p,s\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such
that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(p),\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(s)\in\mathsf{State}
,

p
is the predecessor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}]
,
s
is the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}]
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(p))
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.
We know that
p,s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})
immediately, as
\alpha\subseteq\alpha\oplus\gamma
and
\beta\subseteq\beta\oplus\delta
and for all other nodes the labels are the same.
From
lemma

C.66
, using the identity function as witness to show that
[\mathbf{v}]
is a convex subpomset of
[\mathbf{u}]
, we get that
s
is the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{u}]
, and
p
is the predecessor of
u
in
[\mathbf{u}]
, and we are done.
The case where
u\in S_{\mathbf{w}}
is similar.

(A7)

Let
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
be s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
. Take

v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
. W.l.o.g. let
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
. If
u\neq*_{\min}
and
u\neq*_{\max}
, we
find a path for
v
from
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
to
u
such that
s=*_{\min}
or
s
is the
successor of an assignment-node
a\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
with label
v\leftarrow k
for
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
.
If
s=*_{\min}
, as
\alpha\subseteq\alpha\oplus\gamma
, the path is also a path from
s
to

u
in
[\mathbf{u}]
. If
s\neq*_{\min}
, then we know that
a\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
with label

v\leftarrow k
and
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
. From
lemma

C.66
we know that
s
is the
successor of
a
in
S_{\mathbf{u}}
. We can also immediately infer that the path for
v
from
s

to
u
in
[\mathbf{v}]
is also a path for
v
from
s
to
u
in
[\mathbf{u}]
.
The case where
u=*_{\min}
is trivial. If
u=*_{\max}
, we can assume w.l.o.g. that
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\beta)
, and use our induction
hypothesis to obtain a path for
v
similar to the first case.

For the implication from right to left, we proceed by induction on the size of
U
; this is well-founded, because
U
is series-parallel and therefore finite.
Specifically, our induction hypothesis is that if
V
satisfies
(A1)
–
(A6)
and is strictly smaller than
U
, then
V
is guarded.

Since
U
satisfies
(A1)
, there exist
*_{\min},*_{\max}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
, respectively the
\leq_{\mathbf{u}}
-minimum and
\leq_{\mathbf{u}}
-maximum of
S_{\mathbf{u}}
, such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\min})=\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\max})=\beta\in\mathsf{State}
.
If
*_{\min}=*_{\max}
, we know that
U=\alpha=\beta
, which makes
U
guarded.
Otherwise, we can write
U=\alpha\cdot V\cdot\beta
for some pomset
V
, and note that
V
is series-parallel because
U
is — after all, if
U
does not contain an
\mathsf{N}
-shape, then neither does
V
.
This gives us four cases to consider.

•

If
V=1
, we reach a contradiction, for then
U=\alpha\cdot\beta
, which fails
(A2)
, as there is no action in between the state-labelled nodes
\alpha
and
\beta
.
We can therefore exclude this case.

•

If
V=\mathtt{a}
for some
\mathtt{a}\in\mathsf{Act}\cup\mathsf{State}
, we exclude the possibility that
\mathtt{a}=\gamma\in\mathsf{State}
, for then
U
would again fail
(A2)
.
Hence,
\mathtt{a}\in\mathsf{Act}
. We verify that
\beta=\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
. We have two cases. Suppose first that
a=v\leftarrow n
for some
n\in\textsc{Val}
. Take
v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
. Then if
v=v^{\prime}
, we know that
\beta(v^{\prime})=n
, because of
(A4)
. So then
\beta(v^{\prime})=\alpha[\mathtt{a}](v^{\prime})
.
If
v\neq v^{\prime}
, then we distinguish two cases again. If
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
, then as
U
satisfies
(A3)
, we obtain
\beta(v^{\prime})=\alpha(v^{\prime})
. Hence,
\beta(v^{\prime})=\alpha[\mathtt{a}](v^{\prime})
. If

v^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
, we do a proof by contradiction. Suppose that
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\beta)
. Then because
U

satisfies
(A6)
, as
\mathtt{a}
does not change the value of
v^{\prime}
, we know that there is a path from
\alpha
to
\beta
for
v^{\prime}
. Thus we have that
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
, which is a contradiction. Hence,
v^{\prime}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\beta)
.
We can conclude that
\beta=\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
.

For the second case, suppose that
\mathtt{a}=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
for some
v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
. From
(A5)
, we establish that
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
, and
\beta(v)=\alpha(v^{\prime})=\beta(v^{\prime})
.
Hence,
\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
exists. Take
v^{\prime\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
. If
v=v^{\prime\prime}
, we know that
\beta(v^{\prime\prime})=\alpha(v^{\prime})
, and thus
\beta(v^{\prime\prime})=\alpha[\mathtt{a}](v^{\prime\prime})
.
If
v\neq v^{\prime\prime}
, then we distinguish two cases. If
v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
, as
U
satisfies
(A3)
, we obtain
\beta(v^{\prime\prime})=\alpha(v^{\prime\prime})
. Hence,
\beta(v^{\prime\prime})=\alpha[\mathtt{a}](v^{\prime\prime})
. We can exclude the case where

v^{\prime\prime}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
similarly as above.
Hence,
\beta=\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
and
\alpha[\mathtt{a}]
exists, which makes
U=\alpha\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot\beta
a guarded pomset.

•

Suppose that
V=V_{0}\cdot V_{1}
for some non-empty series-parallel pomsets
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
.
We write
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]=\alpha\cdot V_{0}
and
[\mathbf{v}_{1}]=V_{1}\cdot\beta
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
and
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
are disjoint, and all of the following hold:

S_{\mathbf{u}}=S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\cup S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}}\cup{\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}}\cup S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\times S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=\begin{cases}\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)&u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\\
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(u)&u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\\
\end{cases}

Let
T_{0}\subseteq S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
be the set of
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
-maxima of
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, and let
T_{1}\subseteq S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
be the set of
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
-minima of
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
; note that these sets are non-empty, because
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
are non-empty.
We proceed to make the following observations about the labels of nodes in
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
:

–

Suppose that
v_{0}\in T_{0}
and
v_{1}\in T_{1}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(v_{0})\in\mathsf{State}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(v_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
.
In that case, we know that
v_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}
. Since
U
satisfies
(A2)
, we obtain a
v_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
v_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}v_{2}<_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(v_{2})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
We have now reached a contradiction, for if
v_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
then
v_{0}
is not a
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
-maximum of
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, and if
v_{2}\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
then
v_{1}
is not a
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
-minimum of
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
.
Thus we can conclude that
at most
one of
T_{0},T_{1}
contains a node labelled by an atom.

–

By the above and the fact that
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
are non-empty, it follows that at least one of
T_{0},T_{1}
contains a node labelled by a letter.
For instance, suppose
v_{0}\in T_{0}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(v_{0})\in\mathsf{Act}
.
In that case, since
U
satisfies
(A3)
, we find that there exists a
v_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
v_{1}
is the successor of
v_{0}
, and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(v_{1})\in\mathsf{State}
.
We know that
v_{1}\not\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, for then
v_{0}
would not be
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
-maximal in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, and thus
v_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
.
Furthermore, if
v_{1}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
, then
v_{0}<_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}^{\prime}
, and hence
v_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}^{\prime}
, meaning
v_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}v_{1}^{\prime}
.
This means that
v_{1}
is the unique
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
-minimum of
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
, and hence
T_{1}=\{v_{1}\}
.
A similar analysis applies when
v_{1}\in T_{1}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(v_{1})\in\mathsf{Act}
.

From the above, we learn that either
T_{0}
is a singleton whose only element is state-labelled, and all elements of
T_{1}
are action-labelled, or vice versa.
For the remainder, we assume the former (the dual is argued similarly).
Let
T_{0}=\{*\}
, and choose
\gamma=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*)
; we claim that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
and
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
and are smaller than
U
.

(A3)

For
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
, we first recall that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]=\alpha\cdot V_{0}
, and hence
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
contains a unique state-labelled minimum.
Since
T_{0}=\{*\}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(*)=\gamma\in\mathsf{State}
, we know that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
also contains a unique state-labelled maximum.
Moreover, since
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
, it is straightforward to show that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
is a convex subpomset of
U
; by
lemma

C.70
,
it then follows that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
satisfies
(A2)
and
(A3)
.

(A4)

Take
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u))
.
We immediately obtain
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
,
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
.
As
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A3)
, there exists a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
in
[\mathbf{u}]
.
Since
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
and
*
is the local
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
-maximum of
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
, we know that
u\leq_{\mathbf{u}}*\leq_{\mathbf{u}}*_{\max}
. Suppose there exists
w\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
u\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
.
If
w\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, then
w\leq_{\mathbf{u}}*
. If
w\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
, then
*\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
.
Hence, via
lemma

C.61
we can conclude that
*
is on path
p_{v}
.
From
lemma

C.58
we then know that there exists a path
q_{v}
for
v
from
u
to
*
in
[\mathbf{u}]
. As these are all nodes that also occur in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
,
q_{v}
is also a path for
v
from
u
to
*
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
As
*
is the unique state-labelled maximum of
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
, this proves that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
satisfies
(A3)
.

(A5)

Take
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
for some
v\in\textsc{Var}
and
n\in\textsc{Val}
. Hence,
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
.
As
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A4)
, we obtain a node
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
s
is the successor of
u
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=n
. Because
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
has a maximum state-labelled node, we know that the
successor of
u
in
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
is in fact also a node in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
.
Thus we have
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v)=n
.
From the fact that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
is a convex subpomset of
[\mathbf{u}]
using the identity function and
lemma

C.68
, we can conclude that
s
is a successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
Hence,
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
satisfies
(A4)
.

(A6)

Take
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
for some
v,v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
. Hence,
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
.
Because
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A5)
, we obtain nodes
p,s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
p
is the
predecessor of
u
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p))
,
s
is the successor of
u
and

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.
We know immediately that
p\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
with the same label as in
[\mathbf{u}]
. Because
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
has a maximum state-labelled node, we know that the successor of
u
in
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
is also a node in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
.
Hence,
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.
From
lemma

C.68
, using the identity function as a witness to show that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
is a convex subpomset of
[\mathbf{u}]
, we conclude that
s
and
p
are the successor and predecessor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
Thus
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
satisfies
(A5)
.

(A7)

If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u))
, we immediately obtain
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
,
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and

v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
.
As
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A6)
, there exists a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
to
u
such that either
s=*_{\min}
or
s
is the successor of an assignment-node
a
with label
v\leftarrow k
with
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
.
If
s=*_{\min}
, then, as the minimal node of
[\mathbf{u}]
is the same as the minimal node of
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
and all nodes on path
p_{v}
also occur in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, we can conclude that
p_{v}
is a path from the unique minimum of
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
to
u
for
v
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
If
s\neq*_{\min}
, then we know that
a\leq_{\mathbf{u}}s\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u
, and thus that
s,a\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
. This means that the path
p_{v}
exists entirely out of nodes that are also in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
. Hence we have a path in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
for
v

from
s
to
u
. From
lemma

C.68
, we know that
s
is the successor of
a
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
. Hence,
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
satisfies
(A6)
.

Furthermore, since
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
and
[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
is non-empty, we know that
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
is smaller than
U
.

(A3)

For
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
, we first recall that
[\mathbf{v}_{1}]=V_{1}\cdot\beta
, and hence
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
contains a unique state-labelled maximum.
Furthermore,
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
contains a unique state-labelled minimum by construction, as well.
Since
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
has a unique maximum labelled by
\gamma
, we can write
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]=W\cdot\gamma
for some pomset
W
; moreover,
W
must be non-empty, for otherwise
\alpha\cdot V_{0}=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]=\gamma
, meaning
V_{0}
is empty.
Since
U=[\mathbf{v_{0}}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]=W\cdot\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
, we find that
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
is a convex subpomset of
U
.
By
lemma

C.70
, it then follows that
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfies
(A2)
and
(A3)
.

(A4)

Now consider
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\cup\{*\}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(u))
.
We immediately obtain
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
,
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
.
As
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A3)
, there exists a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
in
[\mathbf{u}]
.
Since
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
, we know the nodes of
p_{v}
all also exist in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
, and the unique state-labelled maximum of
U
,
*_{\max}
, is also the unique state-labelled maximum of
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
.
We immediately obtain that
p_{v}
is a path for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
in
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
. Hence,
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfies
(A3)
.

(A5)

Take
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
for some
v\in\textsc{Var}
and
n\in\textsc{Val}
. Thus
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
.
Because
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A4)
, we obtain a node
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
s
is the successor
of
u
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=n
. We know that
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
. Hence,
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
.
We can conclude that
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(s)(v)=n
.
From
lemma

C.68
, we infer that
s
is also the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
.
Hence,
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfies
(A4)
.

(A6)

Take
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
for some
v,v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
. Thus
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
.
Because
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A5)
, we obtain nodes
p,s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
p
and
s
are respectively the
predecessor and successor of
u
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p))
, and

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.
Immediately we obtain
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
. Because
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
has a minimum state-labelled node, we know that the predecessor of
u
in
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
is also a node in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\cup\{*\}
.
Hence,
p,s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\cup\{*\}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.
From
lemma

C.68
, we infer that
s
and
p
are the successor and predecessor of
u
in
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
.
Thus
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfies
(A5)
.

(A7)

If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\cup\{*\}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(u))
,
we know that
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
,
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and

v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
.
As
[\mathbf{u}]
satisfies
(A6)
, there is a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
to
u
such that either
s=*_{\min}
or
s
is the successor of a node
a
with label
v\leftarrow k
for
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
.
If
s=*_{\min}
, we know that
*_{\min}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}*\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u
. Suppose there exists
w\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
*_{\min}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
.
If
w\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, then since
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
and
*
is the local
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
-maximum of
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
, we obtain
w\leq_{\mathbf{u}}*
.
If
w\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
,
*\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
.
Then we apply
lemma

C.61
to conclude that
*
is on path
p_{v}
.
From
lemma

C.58
we obtain a path
t_{v}
for
v
from
*
to
u
in
[\mathbf{u}]
. As these are all nodes that also occur in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\cup\{*\}
,
t_{v}
is also a path for
v
from
*
to
u
in
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
.
As
*
is the unique state-labelled minimum of
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
, this proves that
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfies
(A6)
.

If
s\neq*_{\min}
, we have two cases. If
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
, because the minimal nodes of
[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
are assignment-labelled, we know that
a
is also a node in
[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
and the path
p_{v}
only contains nodes
that are in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
.
Thus we have a path for
v
from
s
to
u
, with
s
the successor of assignment-node
a
(
lemma

C.68
) and the label of
a
is
v\leftarrow k
. If

s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, then also
a\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
and
s\leq_{\mathbf{u}}*\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u
.
Suppose there exists
w\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
s\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
. If
w\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
, then
w\leq_{\mathbf{u}}*
.
If
w\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
, as
U=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
and
*
is the local
\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
-maximum of
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
, we obtain
*\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
.
We apply
lemma

C.61
to conclude that
*
is on path
p_{v}
.
From
lemma

C.58
we obtain a path
t_{v}
for
v
from
*
to
u
in
[\mathbf{u}]
. As these are all nodes that also occur in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}\cup\{*\}
,
t_{v}
is also a path for
v
from
*
to
u
in
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
.
As
*
is the unique state-labelled minimum of
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
, this proves that also in this case
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfies
(A6)
.

Since
U=W\cdot\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
and
W
is non-empty, we know that
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
is smaller than
U
. Finally, since
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]=W\cdot\gamma
and
\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]
satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
and are strictly smaller than
U
, we can conclude by the induction hypothesis that both are guarded.
This implies that
W\cdot\gamma\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]=[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{1}]=U
is guarded by definition.

•

Suppose that
V=V_{0}\parallel V_{1}
for some non-empty series-parallel pomsets
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
.
As
U
satisfies
(A1)
–
(A6)
, we know that for each
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
there exists a path
p_{v}
from
*_{\min}
(note that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\min})=\alpha
) to
*_{\max}
. As every node on
p_{v}
is
related via
\leq_{\mathbf{u}}
, we know
p_{v}
exists out of nodes from either only
V_{0}
or just
V_{1}
.
This leads to the following definition of
\alpha_{0}
,
\beta_{0}
,
\alpha_{1}
, and
\beta_{1}
. For
v\in\textsc{Var}
:

\alpha_{0}(v)=\begin{cases}\alpha(v)&\exists\text{ a path }p_{v}\text{ from }*_{\min}\text{ to }*_{\max}\text{ in }U\text{ that only uses nodes in }V_{0}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

\beta_{0}(v)=\begin{cases}\beta(v)&\exists u_{0}\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\text{ s.t. }\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u_{0})=v\leftarrow k\\
\beta(v)&\exists\text{ a path }p_{v}\text{ from }*_{\min}\text{ to }*_{\max}\text{ in }U\text{ that only uses nodes in }V_{0}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

We define
\alpha_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
analogously. We claim that
\alpha_{0}\oplus\alpha_{1}
is defined and equal to
\alpha
. For
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha)
, there is a path for
v
from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
in
U
by
(A3)
. This
path runs either through
V_{0}
or
V_{1}
. Hence,
\alpha_{0}(v)=\alpha(v)
or
\alpha_{1}(v)=\alpha(v)
(or both) holds, which implies
\alpha_{0}\oplus\alpha_{1}(v)=\alpha(v)
.
For
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha_{0}\oplus\alpha_{1})
, we know without loss of generality that
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\alpha_{0})
and then by construction we know that
\alpha_{0}\oplus\alpha_{1}(v)=\alpha_{0}(v)=\alpha(v)
.

Similarly, we show that
\beta_{0}\oplus\beta_{1}
is defined and equal to
\beta
. For
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\beta)
, as
U
satisfies
(A6)
, we have two cases. In the first case, there exists a path for
v
from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
that runs either entirely through
V_{0}
or entirely through
V_{1}
. Hence,
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)

or
\beta_{1}(v)=\alpha(v)
(or both) holds, which implies
\beta_{0}\oplus\beta_{1}(v)=\beta(v)
.
In the other case, there exists
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)=v\leftarrow k
or
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}(s)=v\leftarrow k
.
W.l.o.g. we assume the former.
Thus
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)
.
For
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\beta_{0}\oplus\beta_{1})
, we know w.l.o.g. that
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\beta_{0})
and then by construction we know that
\beta_{0}\oplus\beta_{1}(v)=\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)
.

We write
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]=\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
and
[\mathbf{v}_{1}]=\alpha_{1}\cdot V_{1}\cdot\beta_{1}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
S_{\mathbf{u}}=S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}\cup S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
and
{\leq_{\mathbf{u}}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}}\cup{\leq_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}}
,
with
S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}\cap S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}=\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
, with
*_{\min}
the node with label

\alpha_{0}
in
[\mathbf{v_{0}}]
and label
\alpha_{1}
in
[\mathbf{v_{1}}]
and
*_{\max}
the node with label
\beta_{0}
in

[\mathbf{v_{0}}]
and label
\beta_{1}
in
[\mathbf{v_{1}}]
. We also have
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\min})=\alpha

and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(*_{\max})=\beta
and for all nodes
v\in S_{\mathbf{u}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
have
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}(v)

if
v\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}(v)
if
v\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
.

We now argue that
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}\cdot V_{1}\cdot\beta_{1}
satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
.
We only show the argument for
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
, as the proof for
\alpha_{1}\cdot V_{1}\cdot\beta_{1}
is identical.

(A3)

It is immediate that
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
is a convex subpomset of
U
, and hence by
lemma

C.70
,
we can conclude that
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
satisfies
(A2)
and
(A3)
.

(A4)

If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u))
,
we get
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
s.t.
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
.
As
U
satisfies
(A3)
, we obtain a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
.
We distinguish three cases. If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
, then
p_{v}
only uses nodes in
V_{0}
and from
(A6)
we obtain
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)

(there exists a path for
v
from
*_{\min}
to
u
which combined with
p_{v}
forms a path from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
for
v
that uses only nodes in
V_{0}
, or there exists a node
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)=v\leftarrow k
).
This makes
p_{v}
a path for
v
from
u
to
*_{\max}
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
If
u=*_{\min}
, then by construction of
\alpha_{0}
we obtain a path
q_{v}
for
v
from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
in
U
that only uses nodes in
V_{0}
(note that
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u))=\mathsf{dom}(\alpha_{0})
). By definition of
\beta_{0}
we have then that
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)
.
The path
q_{v}
is then immediately a path from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
for
v
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
If
u=*_{\max}
, we get a trivial path from
u
to
*_{\max}
for
v
. Hence,
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
satisfies
(A3)
.

(A5)

If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
and
n\in\textsc{Val}
, then we get
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow n
.
As
U
satisfies
(A4)
, we know that the successor of
u
is
s
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=n
.
We distinguish two cases. The first case is
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
with
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v)=n
. From
lemma

C.68
, we infer that
s
is the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
as well.
In the other case we have
s=*_{\max}
. By definition we have
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)=n
. To see that
\beta_{0}
is the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
, we use
lemma

C.68
.
Hence,
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
satisfies
(A4)
.

(A6)

If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
for
v,v^{\prime}\in\textsc{Var}
, we get
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u)=v\leftarrow v^{\prime}
.
As
U
satisfies
(A5)
, we obtain
p,s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
p
and
s
are resp. the predecessor and successor of
u
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.

–

Let
p\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
. From
lemma

C.68
, we infer that
p

is the predecessor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
as well. We then have two cases. Either
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
with

\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(p)(v^{\prime})
, in which case
via
lemma

C.68
we are done immediately, or
s=*_{\max}
.
In the latter case, by construction of
\beta_{0}
we have
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)
. To see that
\beta_{0}
is the successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
, we use
lemma

C.68
. From
U
satisfying
(A6)
, we infer that there exists
a path
p_{v^{\prime}}
from
w\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
to
p
such that either
w=*_{\min}
or
w
is the successor of
an assignment-node
a
with label
v^{\prime}\leftarrow k
for
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
. In the first case, we obtain a path from
s
to
*_{\max}
which combined with
p_{v^{\prime}}
forms a path from
*_{\min}
to

*_{\max}
for
v^{\prime}
, and all these nodes are in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
. Hence, by construction of
\beta_{0}
we obtain
\beta_{0}(v^{\prime})=\beta(v^{\prime})
. In the second case, we know node
a\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
, and thus
\beta_{0}(v^{\prime})=\beta(v^{\prime})
.
Hence,
\beta_{0}(v^{\prime})=\beta(v^{\prime})=\beta(v)=\beta_{0}(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(p)(v^{\prime})
, and we are done.

–

If
p=*_{\min}
, we have two cases. First, we consider the case where
s\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
. From
lemma

C.68
, we know that

*_{\min}
and
s
are resp. the predecessor and successor of
u
in
[\mathbf{v_{0}}]
. We also have

\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}(s)(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})
. From
U
satisfying
(A3)
, we know there is a
path
p_{v^{\prime}}
from
s
to
*_{\max}
for
v^{\prime}
. This path only uses nodes in
V_{0}
. Then, we know that

p_{v^{\prime}}
together with the node
u
forms a path for
v^{\prime}
from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
using nodes in

V_{0}
. By construction, we have
\alpha_{0}(v^{\prime})=\alpha(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.
In the other case, we have
s=*_{\max}
. By construction we have
\alpha_{0}(v^{\prime})=\alpha(v^{\prime})
, and
\beta_{0}(v^{\prime})=\beta(v^{\prime})
. We also get immediately that
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)
.
Thus we have
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v)=\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)=\beta(v^{\prime})=\beta_{0}(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v^{\prime})
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(s)(v^{\prime})=\beta(v^{\prime})=\alpha(v^{\prime})=\alpha_{0}(v^{\prime})=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(p)(v^{\prime})
.
This concludes the proof that
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
satisfies
(A5)
.

(A7)

If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
s.t.
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u)\in\mathsf{State}
and
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(u))
,
u\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
v\in\mathsf{dom}(\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u))
.
As
U
satisfies
(A6)
, there exists a path
p_{v}
for
v
from
s
to
u
such that
s=*_{\min}
or
s
is the successor of an assignment-node with label
v\leftarrow k
with
k\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
.
In the former case, we distinguish three cases.
If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
, then
p_{v}
runs entirely through
V_{0}
. A
U
satisfies
(A3)
, there exists a path
t_{v}
from
u
to
*_{\max}
using only nodes in
V_{0}
. Combining
p_{v}
and
t_{v}
we obtain a path from

*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
for

v
through
V_{0}
. Then by construction
\alpha_{0}(v)=\alpha(v)
.
This makes
p_{v}
a path for
v
from
\alpha_{0}
to
u
in
V_{0}
.
If
u=*_{\min}
, the case is trivial.
If
u=*_{\max}
, then
\alpha_{0}(v)=\alpha(v)
and
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)
.
This makes
p_{v}
a path for
v
from
*_{\min}
to
u
in
[\mathbf{v_{0}}]
.
In the second case, we distinguish two cases.
If
u\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\setminus\{*_{\min},*_{\max}\}
, then
p_{v}
uses only nodes in
V_{0}
and we are done immediately.
If
u=*_{\max}
then by definition of
\beta_{0}
we have either that there exists a node
w
in
S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(w)=v\leftarrow k
or there exists a path
t_{v}
for
v
from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
using only nodes in
V_{0}
.
In the former case, using the fact that we know that
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
already satisfies
(A4)
, we know the successor of
w
,
y
, is such that

\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}(y)(v)=k
. By
(A3)
, this gives us a path
s_{v}
for
v
from
y
to

\beta_{0}
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
If there exists a path
t_{v}
from
*_{\min}
to
*_{\max}
using only nodes in
V_{0}
, we know that
\alpha_{0}(v)=\alpha(v)
and
\beta_{0}(v)=\beta(v)
, and thus
t_{v}
is a path for
v
from
\alpha_{0}
to
\beta_{0}
in
[\mathbf{v}_{0}]
.
Hence,
\alpha_{0}\cdot[\mathbf{v}_{0}]\cdot\beta_{0}
satisfies
(A6)
.

This makes
\alpha_{0}\cdot V_{0}\cdot\beta_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}\cdot V_{1}\cdot\beta_{1}
satisfy
(A1)
–
(A6)
, and they are strictly smaller than
U
, and hence by the induction hypothesis we know that they are guarded.
This makes
U=\alpha_{0}\oplus\alpha_{1}\cdot(V_{0}\parallel V_{1})\cdot\beta_{0}\oplus\beta_{1}
a guarded pomset by definition.

Appendix D
Proofs about the litmus test

See
8

Proof D.73
.

We prove by contradiction; assume that
P(U)
and that
U
is guarded. Via
section

5
we conclude that
U
satisfies
(A1)
–
(A6)
. From
P(U)
we infer that there
exists
u_{1},u_{2},w\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})=(x\leftarrow 1)
,
u_{1}\leq w
and

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(r_{0})=0=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(r_{1})
.
From
(A2)
and
(A4)
, we infer that
u_{1}
has a unique
successor node
s_{1}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
s_{1}
is state-labelled, and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s_{1})(x)=1
. From
(A3)
there exists a path for
x
from
s_{1}
to
w
. Hence, if

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(x)\neq 1
, there must be at least one assignment between
s_{1}
and
w
altering the
value of
x
, as the path must explain how the value of
x
changed from
1
to
0
. Hence, there exists a node
u_{3}\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
s_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{3}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
and

\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{3})=(x\leftarrow n)
for
n\in\textsc{Var}\cup\textsc{Val}
. However, from property
P
, we know that all such assignments occur before
u_{1}
, and thereby strictly before
s_{1}
.
From this we can conclude that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(x)=1
. Similarly, we obtain
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(y)=1
.

From
(A2)
and
(A5)
, we know that
v_{1}
has a unique successor node
t_{1}
, such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(t_{1})(r_{0})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(t_{1})(y)
.
Then from
(A3)
, there must be a path for
r_{0}
from
t_{1}
to
w
. With similar reasoning as for
x
above, we obtain
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(t_{1})(r_{0})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(r_{0})=0
. Similarly, we obtain a successor node
t_{2}
of
v_{2}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(t_{2})(r_{1})=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(t_{2})(x)=0
.

As we have that
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(t_{1})(y)=0
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(w)(y)=1
and
t_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
, we can
conclude from
(A3)
that there must be a path from
t_{1}
to
w
for
y
such that
this path contains at least one assignment that alters the value for
y
. Thus, there exists a node

u_{3}
such that
t_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{3}\leq_{\mathbf{w}}w
and
u_{3}
has a label that changes the value of
y
. Similarly, we obtain a node
u_{4}
such that
t_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{4}\leq_{\mathbf{w}}w
and
u_{4}
changes the value of

x
. From property
P
, we obtain
u_{3}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}
and
u_{4}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.
Then, making use of the fact that
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are the successors of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
respectively, we can derive:

v_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}t_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{4}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}t_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{3}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{2}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{2}

Then, by antisymmetry, all these nodes are equivalent. As they cannot be, we have a contradiction. Hence,
U
is not a guarded pomset. Hence,
U
is not a guarded pomset.

To prove
9
, we must first consider a series of auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma D.74
.

Let
U_{0},U_{1},V\in\mathsf{SP}
. If
U_{0}\cdot U_{1}\preceq V
, then there exist
V_{0},V_{1}\in\mathsf{SP}
such that

V=V_{0}\cdot V_{1}U_{0}\preceq V_{0}U_{1}\preceq V_{1}

Proof D.75
.

We write
V=[\mathbf{v}]
,
U_{0}=[\mathbf{u_{0}}]
and
U_{1}=[\mathbf{u_{1}}]
.
For
i\in\{0,1\}
, we choose
V_{i}=[\mathbf{v}_{i}]
by

S_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}=\{v\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\;\;|\;\;h(v)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{i}}}\}{\leq_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v}}}\cap S_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}^{2}\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v)

where
h:S_{\mathbf{v}}\to S_{\mathbf{u}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}}
witnesses that
U_{0}\cdot U_{1}\preceq V
.
We first verify whether
V=V_{0}\cdot V_{1}
.

•

For the carrier, take
x\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
.
This establishes that
h(x)\in S_{\mathbf{u}_{0}}
or
h(x)\in S_{\mathbf{u}_{1}}
.
Hence,
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
or
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
.
The converse inclusion holds by construction.

•

To check that
{\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}\cdot\mathbf{v_{1}}}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v}}}
, first suppose that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}}y
.
We have three cases to distinguish.

–

If
h(x),h(y)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
, then
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
and we immediately obtain that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}y
and thus that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}}y
.
Same for
h(x),h(y)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{1}}}
.

–

On the other hand, if
h(x)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{1}}}
and
h(y)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
, then
h(y)\leq_{\mathbf{u}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}}h(x)
.
Since
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}}h(y)
already, this implies that
h(x)=h(y)
.
But then
h(x)\in S_{\mathbf{u}_{0}}
, which contradicts that
S_{\mathbf{u}_{0}}
is disjoint from
S_{\mathbf{u}_{1}}
.
We can therefore disregard this case.

–

This leaves the last possibility where
h(x)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
and
h(y)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{1}}}
.
This establishes that
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
and
y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
, and we obtain that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}}y
.

Conversely, if
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}}y
, then we distinguish two possibilities.

–

If
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
, then
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}y
so
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}}y
immediately, and similarly when
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
.

–

On the other hand, if
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
and
y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
, then
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}\cdot u_{1}}}h(y)
.
This means by properties of
\preceq
that either
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}}y
, in which case we are done, or
y\leq_{\mathbf{v}}x
.
In the latter case, we obtain that
h(y)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}\cdot u_{1}}}h(x)
, which results in
h(x)=h(y)
.
This contradicts the fact that
S_{\mathbf{u}_{0}}
is disjoint from
S_{\mathbf{u}_{1}}
, which means that we can disregard this case.

•

For the labels it follows immediately that
[\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}]
gives the same labels as
[\mathbf{v}]
.

The next thing to show is that
U_{0}\preceq V_{0}
and
U_{1}\preceq V_{1}
.
Both cases are similar, so we only prove
U_{0}\preceq V_{0}
.
We take as a witness the function
h_{0}:S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\to S_{\mathbf{u}_{0}}
given by
h_{0}(x)=h(x)
.

•

First we prove that this is a surjective function from
V_{0}
to
U_{0}
.
For
v\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
, we know that
h_{0}(v)=h(v)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
by construction.
For
u\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
, we know there exists
v\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
such that
h(v)=u
by surjectivity of
h
on
U_{0}\cdot U_{1}
.
Suppose that
v\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
.
By construction of
S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
this means that
h(v)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{1}}}
.
This is a contradiction, thus we must have that
v\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
, and we conclude that
h
is also surjective when restricted to
S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
.

•

If
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}y
, then
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}}y
thus
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}\cdot u_{1}}}h(y)
.
Since both
h(x),h(y)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
, we obtain that
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}h(y)
.

•

If
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}h(y)
, then
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
. If
\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}(x)\in\mathsf{Act}
or
\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}(y)\in\mathsf{Act}
, then
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(x)\in\mathsf{Act}
or
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(y)\in\mathsf{Act}
.
From
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}h(y)
we obtain that
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}\cdot u_{1}}}h(y)
.
Thus it follows that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}}y
.
Since
h(x),h(y)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
, we know that
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
, and we can conlude
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}y
.

•

The last requirement (if
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}h(y)
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}(x),\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}(y)\in\mathsf{State}
, then
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}y
or
y\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}x
) is checked similarly.

Lemma D.76
.

Let
U_{0},U_{1},V\in\mathsf{SP}
. If
U_{0}\parallel U_{1}\preceq V
, then there exist
V_{0},V_{1}\in\mathsf{SP}
such that

V=V_{0}\parallel V_{1}U_{0}\preceq V_{0}U_{1}\preceq V_{1}

Proof D.77
.

We write
V=[\mathbf{v}]
,
U_{0}=[\mathbf{u_{0}}]
and
U_{1}=[\mathbf{u_{1}}]
.
For
i\in\{0,1\}
, we choose
V_{i}=[\mathbf{v}_{i}]
by

S_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}=\{v\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\;\;|\;\;h(v)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{i}}}\}{\leq_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v}}}\cap S_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}^{2}\lambda_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}(v)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}}(v)

where
h:S_{\mathbf{v}}\to S_{\mathbf{u}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}}
witnesses that
U_{0}\cdot U_{1}\preceq V
.
We first verify whether
V=V_{0}\parallel V_{1}
.

•

Take
x\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
.
Then we know that
h(x)
in
U_{0}
or in
U_{1}
from
U_{1}\parallel U_{2}\preceq V
. Hence, we can conclude that
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
or
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
.
The converse inclusion holds by construction.

•

To check that
{\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\parallel v_{1}}}}={\leq_{\mathbf{v}}}
we check both directions.
If
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}}y
, then we know that
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}}\parallel\mathbf{u_{1}}}h(y)
.
In that case
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u}_{i}}h(y)
for
i\in\{0,1\}
.
In that case we know that
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{i}}
and we immediately obtain that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}y
and thus that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{i}\parallel v_{i}}}y
.
The converse claim, i.e., that whenever
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\parallel\mathbf{v}_{1}}y
also
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}}y
holds by construction.

•

For the labels it follows immediately that
[\mathbf{v_{0}\parallel v_{1}}]
gives the same labels as
[\mathbf{v}]
.

It remains to show
U_{0}\preceq V_{0}
and
U_{1}\preceq V_{1}
; this is analagous to the proof of
lemma

D.74
.

Lemma D.78
.

Let
\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}
be
\preceq
restricted to
\mathsf{SP}
.
Then
\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}
is the smallest precongruence (preorder monotone w.r.t. the operators) such that for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
we have that
\alpha\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}\alpha\cdot\alpha
.

Proof D.79
.

We first show that
\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}
is a preorder monotone w.r.t. the operators. Thus we need to prove that
\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}
is reflexive, transitive and monotone. Reflexivity follows immediately by using the identity function as a witness, and transivity by
sing function composition.

For monotonicity we check whether for
U_{0}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}
and
U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{1}
we have
U_{0}\cdot U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}\cdot V_{1}
and
U_{0}\parallel U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}\parallel V_{1}
.
For
i\in\{0,1\}
, let
U_{i}=[\mathbf{u}_{i}]
and
V_{i}=[\mathbf{v}_{i}]
, and let
h_{i}:S_{\mathbf{v}_{i}}\to S_{\mathbf{u}_{i}}
be the function witnessing that
U_{i}\preceq V_{i}
.
We choose
h
as the union of
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
.
To see that
h
witnesses that
U_{0}\cdot U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}\cdot V_{1}
, we have to check the conditions of
definition

6.37
.

•

On the one hand, if
v\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}}v^{\prime}
because
v\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
and
v^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
, we immediately obtain that
h(v)=h_{0}(v)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}\cdot u_{1}}}h_{1}(v^{\prime})=h(v^{\prime})
, as we know that
h_{0}(v)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{0}}}
and
h_{1}(v^{\prime})\in S_{\mathbf{u_{1}}}
.

On the other hand, if
v\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}}v^{\prime}
because
v,v^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
, we can establish immediately that
h_{i}(v)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{i}}}h_{i}(v^{\prime})
and thus
h(v)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{0}\cdot u_{1}}}h(v^{\prime})
.

•

Next, suppose that
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\cup S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
such that
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}}h(y)
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}(x),\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}(y)\in\mathsf{State}
.
We should show that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}y
or
y\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}x
.
We distinguish three cases:

–

If
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
and
y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
, then we know immediately that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}}y
and we are done.

–

If
x\in S_{\mathbf{v_{1}}}
and
y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{0}}}
, we have
y\leq_{\mathbf{v_{0}\cdot v_{1}}}x
and we are also done.

–

If
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}
for some
i\in\{0,1\}
, then we know that
h(x),h(y)\in S_{\mathbf{u_{i}}}
, and thus that
h_{i}(v)\leq_{\mathbf{u_{i}}}h_{i}(v^{\prime})
.
As
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{i}}(x)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}(x)
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{i}}(y)=\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}(y)
, we can establish that
x\leq_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}y
or
y\leq_{\mathbf{v_{i}}}x
.
Hence,
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}y
or
y\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}x
.

•

Now uppose that
x,y\in S_{\mathbf{v}_{0}}\cup S_{\mathbf{v}_{1}}
such that
h(x)\leq_{\mathbf{u}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{1}}h(y)
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}(x)\in\mathsf{Act}
or
\lambda_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}(y)\in\mathsf{Act}
.
By an argument similar to the previous case, we can argue
x\leq_{\mathbf{v}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{1}}y
.

•

The labelling requirements are satisfied immediately.

A similar argument can be used to show that
h
witnesses
U_{0}\parallel U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}\parallel V_{1}
.

The last thing to check is whether for
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
we have that
\alpha\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}\alpha\cdot\alpha
.
This is witnessed by the unique function from the carrier of the latter to that of the former.

Next thing to show is that
\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}
is the smallest preorder for which all of the conditions hold.
Suppose we have another preorder
\triangleleft
that fulfills the conditions.
We now need to show that if
U\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V
, then
U\triangleleft V
.
We do this by induction on
U
, as
U
is a series-parallel pomset.
In the base we have three cases to consider.

•

If
U=1
, then
U\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V
means that
V=1
, and thus
U\triangleleft V
.

•

If
U=\mathtt{a}
for
\mathtt{a}\in\mathsf{Act}
,
U\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V
can only hold when
V=a
, and thus
U\triangleleft V
again.

•

If
U=\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
, then
U\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V
implies that
V=\alpha^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
.
A straightforward argument by induction on
n
then shows that
U\triangleleft V
.

In the inductive step we have two cases.

•

If
U=U_{0}\cdot U_{1}
and
U\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V
.
We know from
lemma

D.74
that
V=V_{0}\cdot V_{1}
and
U_{0}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}
and
U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{1}
.
From the induction hypothesis we obtain that
U_{0}\triangleleft V_{0}
and
U_{1}\triangleleft V_{1}
.
As
\triangleleft
is monotone w.r.t the operators, we obtain that
U\triangleleft V
.

•

If
U=U_{0}\parallel U_{1}
and
U\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V
.
We know from
lemma

D.76
that
V=V_{0}\parallel V_{1}
and
U_{0}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}
and
U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{1}
.
From the induction hypothesis we obtain that
U_{0}\triangleleft V_{0}
and
U_{1}\triangleleft V_{1}
.
As
\triangleleft
is monotone w.r.t. the operators, we obtain that
U\triangleleft V
.

Using these lemmas, we can now prove
9
.

See
9

Proof D.80
.

For the implication from left to right, we write
L\preceq K
if for every
U\in L
, there exists
V\in K
such that
U\preceq V
.
We then reformulate the statement as

\forall A\subseteq{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}},\text{ }A\preceq L.

We proceed by induction on the construction of
{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.

•

The base case is
A=L
, this one is trivial as
\preceq
is reflexive.

•

For the inductive step we have
A=C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\right\rrbracket}]
for
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
and
C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\cdot\alpha\right\rrbracket}]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.
From the induction hypothesis we obtain
C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\cdot\alpha\right\rrbracket}]\preceq L
.
Now, if
U\in C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\right\rrbracket}]
, then
U=C[\alpha]
.
Since
C[\alpha]\preceq C[\alpha\cdot\alpha]\in C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\cdot\alpha\right\rrbracket}]
, it follows that
C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\right\rrbracket}]\preceq C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\cdot\alpha\right\rrbracket}]
; by transitivity, we then have that
A=C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\right\rrbracket}]\preceq L
.

For the direction from right to left, we first prove that if
C\in\mathsf{PC}
and
U,V\in\mathsf{SP}
such that
U\preceq V
and
C[V]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
, then
C[U]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
, by induction on the construction of
\preceq
as characterised in
lemma

D.78
.
In the base, there are two cases.

•

If
U\preceq V
, because
U=V
, we find that
C[U]=C[V]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
immediately.

•

If
U\preceq V
because
U=\alpha
and
V=\alpha\cdot\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{State}
, then
C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\cdot\alpha\right\rrbracket}]\subseteq{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
, which means that
C[U]\in C[{\left\llbracket\alpha\right\rrbracket}]\subseteq{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.

For the inductive step, there are three cases to consider.

•

If
U\preceq V
because
U=U_{0}\cdot U_{1}
and
V=V_{0}\cdot V_{1}
with
U_{0}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{0}
and
U_{1}\preceq^{\mathsf{SP}}V_{1}
, then first choose
C^{\prime}=C[*\cdot V_{1}]
.
Thus
C^{\prime}[V_{0}]=C[V_{0}\cdot V_{1}]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.
From the induction hypothesis we obtain that
C^{\prime}[U_{0}]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
and
C^{\prime}[U_{0}]=C[U_{0}\cdot V_{1}]
.
Then take
C^{\prime\prime}=C[U_{0}\cdot*]
. Thus
C^{\prime\prime}[V_{1}]=C[U_{0}\cdot V_{1}]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.
Again from the induction hypothesis we get that
C^{\prime\prime}[U_{1}]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
, and
C^{\prime\prime}[U_{1}]=C[U_{0}\cdot U_{1}]=C[U]
.

•

If
U\preceq V
because
U=U_{0}\parallel U_{1}
and
V=V_{0}\parallel V_{1}
with
U_{0}\preceq^{SP}V_{0}
and
U_{1}\preceq^{SP}V_{1}
, the proof proceeds as above.

•

If
U\preceq V
because there exists a
W\in\mathsf{Pom}
and
U\preceq W
and
W\preceq V
, then by the induction hypothesis we find that
C[W]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.
By applying the induction hypothesis again we can conclude that
C[U]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
.

Then if
V\in L
s.t.
U\preceq V
, we can choose
C=*
to find that
C[V]=V\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
, and thus
U=C[U]\in{L}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
, using the previously derived fact.

See
10

Proof D.81
.

First, note that
\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}\cup\mathsf{contr}}}=\left({\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}}}\right){\downarrow^{\mathsf{contr}}}
by
lemma

B.47
.
Thus, if
V\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\downarrow}
, we apply
9
, to infer that there exists a pomset
W\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}}{\downarrow^{\mathsf{exch}}}
such that

V\preceq W
.
Next we can apply
lemma

2.10
, to obtain a pomset
U\in\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\llparenthesis$}}}}}\displaystyle\makebox[0.0pt][c]{\color[rgb]{1,1,1} $\beta$}{e}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\displaystyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{491520}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\textstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{344064}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\resizebox{245760}{582542}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rrparenthesis$}}}}}
such that
W\sqsubseteq U
. We know that
U
has property
P
. We first show that
W
also has property
P
, and then that the same holds for
V
. From the definition of
\sqsubseteq
we get that there exists a bijective pomset morphism
h
from
W
to
U
.
Thus we have
U=[\mathbf{u}]
and
W=[\mathbf{w}]
and a bijective function
h\colon S_{\mathbf{u}}\to S_{\mathbf{w}}
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{w}}\circ h=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}
and
if
u\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u^{\prime}
then
h(u)\leq_{\mathbf{w}}h(u^{\prime})
. Now we need to verify the two properties of
definition

6.36
.

1.

As
\lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(h(u_{1}))=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(u_{1})
, we get

\lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(h(u_{1}))=(x\leftarrow 1)
. The same for the other existential statements of

item

1
. For the ordering: from
u_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}v_{1}\leq_{\mathbf{u}}w
we immediately obtain
that
h(u_{1})\leq_{\mathbf{w}}h(v_{1})\leq_{\mathbf{w}}h(w)
and similarly for

h(u_{2})\leq_{\mathbf{w}}h(v_{2})\leq_{\mathbf{w}}h(w)
.

2.

Take a
z
such that
\lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(z)=(x\leftarrow n)
for
n\in\textsc{Val}\cup\textsc{Var}
. As
h
is surjective, we know there exists a node
s\in S_{\mathbf{u}}
such that
h(s)=z
and
\lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(h(s))=\lambda_{\mathbf{u}}(s)
. As
P(U)
, we get that
s\leq_{\mathbf{u}}u_{1}
.
Hence,
h(s)\leq_{\mathbf{w}}h(u_{1})
and thus
z\leq_{\mathbf{w}}h(u_{1})
. An analogue argument can be given for the other conditions in property (
2
).

This demonstrates that
W
has property
P
. We know that
V\preceq W
, and we will show this implies that
V
also has property
P
. From the definition of
\preceq
we know that there exists a pomset morphism
h
from
V
to
W
. The argument to verify the two properties of
definition

6.36
is exactly the same as above. Hence we can conclude that
V
has property
P
.
